On the Bloch-Kato Conjecture for some four-dimensional symplectic Galois representations
Naomi Sweeting

Abstract
. The Bloch-Kato conjecture predicts a far-reaching connection between orders of vanishing of L L -functions and the ranks of Selmer groups of p p -adic Galois representations. In this article, we consider the four-dimensional, symplectic Galois representations arising from automorphic representations π \pi of GSp 4 ⁡ ( 𝔸 ℚ ) \GSP_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}) with trivial central character and with the lowest cohomological archimedean weight. Under mild technical conditions, we prove that the Selmer group vanishes when the central value L ⁡ ( π , spin , 1 / 2 ) L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2) is nonzero. In the spirit of bipartite Euler systems, we bound the Selmer group by using level-raising congruences to construct ramified Galois cohomology classes. The relation to L L -values comes via the GSpin 3 ↪ GSpin 5 \spin_{3}\hookrightarrow\spin_{5} periods on a compact inner form of GSp 4 \GSP_{4} . We also prove a result towards the rank-one case: if the π \pi -isotypic part of the Abel-Jacobi image of any of Kudla’s one-cycles on the Siegel threefold is nonzero, it generates the full Selmer group. These cycles are linear combinations of embedded quaternionic Shimura curves, and under the conjectural arithmetic Rallis inner product formula, their heights are related to L ′ ​ ( π , spin , 1 / 2 ) L^{\prime}(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2) .



0.
Introduction

0.1.
Main results

Let
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}\pi_{v}
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
of trivial central character and conductor
N(\pi)
, such that
\pi_{\infty}
belongs to the discrete series
L
-packet of parallel weight
(3,3)
. In particular,
\pi
appears in the étale cohomology of the
\GSP_{4}
Shimura variety with trivial coefficients. Let
E
be a sufficiently large coefficient field; then we have a compatible family of
p
-adic Galois representations

\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GSP_{4}(E_{\mathfrak{p}})

indexed by primes
\mathfrak{p}|p
of
E
, and normalized so that the similitude character of
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is cyclotomic. Let
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
be the underlying four-dimensional
E_{\mathfrak{p}}[G_{\mathbb{Q}}]
-module of
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
, and consider the Bloch-Kato Selmer group

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\otimes B_{\operatorname{cris}})\times\prod_{\ell\neq p}H^{1}(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})\right),

where
I_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
is the local inertia subgroup.
The Bloch-Kato conjecture applied to
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
predicts

\dim_{E_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})={\operatorname{ord}}_{s=1/2}L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2),

where the
L
-function is normalized so that
s=1/2
is the central value. Our first main result proves many cases of the Bloch-Kato conjecture for
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
in rank zero.

Theorem A

(Theorem
9.1.4
)
.

Suppose
\pi
is not CAP or endoscopic, and for some
\ell|N(\pi)
,
\pi_{\ell}
has a local Jacquet-Langlands transfer to the compact inner form of
\GSP_{4,\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}|p
be a prime of
E
such that:

(1)

\pi_{p}
is unramified.

(2)

The residual representation
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is absolutely irreducible and generic (Definition
2.7.3
).

(3)

There exists a prime
q\nmid N(\pi)
such that
q^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
, and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}(\Frob_{q})
has eigenvalues

\left\{q,1,\alpha,q/\alpha\right\}

with
\alpha\not\in\left\{\pm 1,\pm q,q^{2},q^{-1}\right\}
.

Then

L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)\neq 0\implies H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=0.

Remarks
.

(i)

The conditions (
1
) and (
2
) are always satisfied for cofinitely many
\mathfrak{p}
. Condition (
3
) is always satisfied for the primes
\mathfrak{p}
lying over a set of rational primes of positive Dirichlet density; it is satisfied for all but finitely many
\mathfrak{p}
under conditions listed in Theorem
C.4.10
.

(ii)

When
p>5
, which is necessary for (
3
),
\pi
is CAP or endoscopic if and only if
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is reducible.

(iii)

For all but finitely many of the primes
\mathfrak{p}
satisfying the conditions in Theorem
A
, we are able to strengthen the result to the
vanishing of the dual Selmer group
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}/T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})
, where
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\subset V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is a Galois-stable lattice; see Definition
9.3.7
and Corollary
9.3.9
.

For instance, from Theorem
A
we can deduce the following:

Corollary B

(Corollary
9.3.10
)
.

Suppose
\pi
is not CAP or endoscopic, and there exists a prime
\ell|N(\pi)
such that
\pi_{\ell}
is of type IIa in the notation of
[
96
]
. Then

L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)\neq 0\implies H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}/T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=0

for all but finitely many
\mathfrak{p}
.

In §
9.4
, we give applications of Theorem
A
to certain
\pi
arising by automorphic induction; this gives new results towards Bloch-Kato for Hilbert modular forms over real quadratic fields of non-parallel weight
(2,4)
(including CM forms), and for twists of classical modular forms of weight 3 by certain Hecke characters of imaginary quadratic fields.

Our second main result concerns the rank one case of Bloch-Kato for
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
. To state it,
let
V
be a quadratic space of signature
(3,2)
, and suppose
\pi
admits a Jacquet-Langlands transfer
\Pi
to the inner form
\spin(V)
of
\GSP_{4}
; for instance, if
V
is split, then
\spin(V)=\GSP_{4}
and we can take
\Pi=\pi
. Let
K\subset\spin(V)(\mathbb{A}_{f})
be a neat compact open subgroup such that
\Pi_{f}^{K}\neq 0
, and let
\Sh_{K}(V)
be the Shimura variety for
\spin(V)
at level
K
, which is a classical Siegel threefold when
V
is split.
Let
\mathfrak{p}|p
be a prime of
E
; then we have

(0.1)

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{\mathfrak{p}})[\Pi_{f}]=\begin{cases}\Pi_{f}^{K}\otimes V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}(-2),&i=3,\\
0,&i\neq 3.\end{cases}

In particular, if
\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi_{f}}}
denotes the Chow group of codimension-two algebraic cycles, localized at the maximal ideal of an appropriate Hecke algebra corresponding to the eigenvalues of
\pi
, we have an Abel-Jacobi map

(0.2)

\begin{split}\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},{\Pi_{f}}}:\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi_{f}}}&\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))[\Pi_{f}])\\
&=H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})\otimes\Pi_{f}^{K}.\end{split}

We consider the codimension-two Kudla cycles

(0.3)

Z(T,\varphi)\in\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V)),

which are indexed by the data of a
2\times 2
symmetric, positive-semidefinite matrix
T
and a
K
-invariant test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z}).

When
T
is nondegenerate,
Z(T,\varphi)
is a linear combination of Shimura curves over
\mathbb{Q}
, embedded into
\Sh_{K}(V)
by
\spin(V)(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-translates of group maps of the form

\spin(1,2)\hookrightarrow\spin(3,2);

conversely, any such embedded Shimura curve can be written as a Kudla cycle for some
T
and
\varphi
.

Theorem C

(Theorem
12.3.1
)
.

Suppose
\pi
is not CAP or endoscopic, and its base change to
\GL_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
does not arise by automorphic induction from an imaginary quadratic or quartic CM field.
Let
\mathfrak{p}|p
be a prime of
E
satisfying (
1
)-(
3
) from Theorem
A
.
Then for any Kudla cycle
Z(T,\varphi)\in\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))
,

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\Pi_{f}}(Z(T,\varphi))\neq 0\implies\dim_{E_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=1.

We now explain the relation of the Bloch-Kato Conjecture to Theorem
C
, in which
L
-values do not directly appear. According to the resolution by Bruinier and Westerholt-Raum of Kudla’s modularity conjecture
[
15
]
, the formal
q
-series

(0.4)

\Theta_{\varphi}\coloneqq\sum_{T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}}Z(T,\varphi)q^{T}

lies in
\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M_{5/2,2}
,
where
M_{5/2,2}
is the space of holomorphic Siegel modular forms
of degree 2 and weight 5/2. Let
f\in M_{5/2,2}
generate the automorphic representation of the metaplectic group
\MP_{4}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
corresponding to
\Pi
under the generalized Shimura-Waldspurger correspondence of Gan-Li
[
34
]
; then the Petersson inner product
\Theta_{\varphi}(f)\coloneqq\langle\Theta_{\varphi},f\rangle
lies in
\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))_{\mathbb{C}}
. The still-conjectural
1
1

1

But see the important progress towards this result by Li-Zhang
[
64
]
, and the unitary case proved by Li-Liu
[
62
,
63
]
.

arithmetic Rallis inner product formula
proposes that, up to some local factors depending on
\varphi
and
f
,
the derivative
L^{\prime}(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)
is related to the height of the cycle
\Theta_{\varphi}(f)
(suitably interpreted). Assuming Beilinson’s conjecture on the injectivity of the
p
-adic Abel-Jacobi map, Theorem
C
could then be reformulated as

L^{\prime}(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)\neq 0\implies\dim_{E_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=1,

which is consistent with the Bloch-Kato conjecture.

0.2.
Overview of the proofs

Before giving more detailed sketches below, we briefly indicate the methods of proof of Theorems
A
and
C
. Pursuing a strategy initiated by Bertolini and Darmon for elliptic curves
[
7
]
– and later extended to many new contexts by other authors

[
24
,
27
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
121
]
– we bound the Selmer group by constructing ramified Galois cohomology classes through level-raising congruences and special cycles on Shimura varieties for ramified
\spin_{5}
groups. Proving the classes we construct are ramified is the most delicate part: by a calculation on the special fiber, the ramification is essentially measured by linear combinations of compact
\spin_{3}-\spin_{5}
periods for a Jacquet-Langlands transfer of
\pi
.
2
2

2

Or a congruent automorphic form to
\pi
, in the case of Theorem
C
.
To access the underlying representation theory of these periods and relate them to
L
-values, we interpret them as the Fourier coefficients of certain theta lifts in
M_{5/2,2}
.

0.3.
Sketch of the proof in the rank zero case

Let us explain the main ideas involved in the proof of Theorem
A
. Fix a
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-stable lattice
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\subset V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
, and let
T_{\pi,n}\coloneqq T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{p}^{n}T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
for
n\geq 1
. The mechanism for bounding the Selmer group is a collection of auxiliary Galois cohomology classes

\kappa_{n}(q)\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n}),

indexed by primes
q
satisfying appropriate level-raising congruence conditions modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
, and
having the following two properties:

(1)

The restriction
\Res_{\ell}\kappa_{n}(q)
is unramified for all
\ell\nmid N(\pi)pq
, and crystalline for
\ell=p
.

(2)

Under the assumption
L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)\neq 0
,
\Res_{q}\kappa_{n}(q)
is ramified (if
n
is sufficiently large).

Given such a system of classes, a standard argument using Poitou-Tate duality shows that
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})
vanishes.

As indicated in §
0.2
, we obtain the classes
\kappa_{n}(q)
by level-raising congruences from special cycles on Shimura varieties for ramified
\spin_{5}
groups. More precisely, fix the prime
\ell|N(\pi)
such that
\pi_{\ell}
has a local Jacquet-Langlands transfer (we will see later that this is crucial for the argument). Let
V_{q\ell}
be the unique five-dimensional quadratic space of signature
(3,2)
and trivial discriminant that ramifies precisely at
q
and
\ell
; one exists by the local-global classification of quadratic forms. On the corresponding
\spin
Shimura variety
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
we have the codimension-two Kudla cycles
Z(T,\varphi)\in\CH^{2}(\Sh(V_{q\ell}))
as in §
0.1
. (For simplicity, we suppress the choice of level structure.)
After localizing at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of the Hecke algebra corresponding to
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
, the main result of
[
43
]
implies that
H^{4}(\Sh(V_{q\ell}),O_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
, where
O\subset E
is the ring of integers – this is the most crucial way that the condition (
2
) of Theorem
A
enters the argument. In particular, one has an Abel-Jacobi map

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}:\CH^{2}(\Sh(V_{q\ell}))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}).

However, it is no longer true that
V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
appears in the étale cohomology
H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{\mathfrak{p}})
, because, as
\pi_{q}
is unramified,
\pi
does not have a Jacquet-Langlands transfer to
\spin(V_{q\ell})
.

Instead, we construct
level-raising maps
(see below)

\alpha_{n}:H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to T_{\pi,n}.

Then we obtain a family of Galois cohomology classes

\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi,\alpha_{n})\coloneqq\alpha_{n}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi))\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n}),

for varying
T
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{q\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})
. Any
\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi,\alpha_{n})
will satisfy
the property (1) above, as long as the level structure for
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
is chosen to be hyperspecial outside
N(\pi)q
. The proof of (2) is far more subtle, as we explain below, and we are only able to show that
some

\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi,\alpha_{n})
is ramified at
q
; in particular, the choice of
T
cannot be made explicit. Once we have any ramified class, however, we can use it as the class called
\kappa_{n}(q)
above.

0.3.1.
Ramification and the relation to
L
-values

Let
V_{\ell}
be the unique five-dimensional, positive-definite quadratic space of trivial discriminant that ramifies only at
\ell
. By the assumption that
\pi_{\ell}
has a local Jacquet-Langlands transfer, one can find a function

(0.5)

\beta_{\pi}:\spin(V_{\ell})(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\spin(V_{\ell})(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K\to O

with the same spherical Hecke eigenvalues as
\pi
, where
K
is a sufficiently small compact open subgroup, with
K_{q}
hyperspecial. We abbreviate this double coset space as
\Sh(V_{\ell})
. Because
\pi
has trivial central character,
\beta_{\pi}
descends to an automorphic form on
\SO(V_{\ell})
; in particular,
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})
, one can consider the classical theta lift

\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi})\in M_{5/2,2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}O.

The classical Rallis inner product formula computes the Petersson norm of
\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi})
:

(0.6)

\langle\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi}),\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi})\rangle\doteq\langle\beta_{\pi},\beta_{\pi}\rangle\cdot L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)

up to local factors depending on
\varphi
and
\beta_{\pi}
.

On the other hand, the Fourier coefficients of
\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi})
can be computed as
\spin_{3}
-periods of
\beta_{\pi}
. In fact, for
T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})
, one can define
Z(T,\varphi)\in\mathbb{Z}[\Sh(V_{\ell})]
analogously to Kudla’s cycles in (
0.3
), arising from group embeddings
\spin_{3}\hookrightarrow\spin(V_{\ell})
. Then we have:

\Theta_{\varphi}(\beta_{\pi})=\sum_{T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}}\beta_{\pi}(Z(T,\varphi))q^{T}.

In particular,

(0.7)

L(\pi,\operatorname{spin},1/2)\neq 0\iff\;\exists\,T,\varphi\text{ s.t. }\beta_{\pi}(Z(T,\varphi))\neq 0.

The connection to Galois cohomology classes comes from the following (idealized) identity, for a certain choice of
\alpha_{n}
:

(0.8)

\Res_{q}\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}},\alpha_{n})=\beta_{\pi}(Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{{\operatorname{unr}}}))\pmod{\mathfrak{p}^{n}}.

Here
\varphi^{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{q\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q},\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q},\mathbb{Z})
is any test function,
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{q\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{Z})
is designed so that
Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}})\in\CH^{2}(\Sh(V_{q\ell}))
will have a reasonable integral model over
\mathbb{Z}_{(q)}
– in fact,
Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}})
is a linear combination of quaternionic Shimura curves ramified at
q
, with the usual maximal compact level structure – and
\varphi_{q}^{{\operatorname{unr}}}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{Z})
is an explicit test function whose origin is described in the next paragraph.
Finally, to make sense of the identity (
0.8
), we use that
H^{1}(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}},T_{\pi,n})^{\Frob_{q}=1}
is free of rank one over
O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
, by the congruence conditions imposed on
q
; so we are viewing both sides as elements of
O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
.

The main tool for proving (
0.8
) is an analysis of the weight spectral sequence for a semistable integral model of
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
over
\mathbb{Z}_{(q)}
; this is obtained by blowup from the canonical PEL model, which has ordinary quadratic singularities. The double coset space
\Sh(V_{\ell})
enters the picture as the indexing set for the irreducible components of the (two-dimensional) supersingular locus of the special fiber of
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
, and indeed
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{unr}}
arises from considering the intersections of the special fiber of
Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}})
with various strata in the supersingular locus.
(Of course, for this discussion to be accurate, the level structures for
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
and
\Sh(V_{\ell})
are chosen to be compatible away from
q
.)

However, even once we have proved (
0.8
), one major obstacle remains to proving that at least one of the classes
\Res_{q}\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}},\alpha_{n})
is ramified. From (
0.7
), one can deduce that there exists
\varphi^{q}
and
T
such that
\beta_{\pi}(Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{sph}}))\neq 0\pmod{\mathfrak{p}^{n}}
for sufficiently large
n
, where
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{sph}}
is the indicator function of a self-dual lattice. Unfortunately, although
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{unr}}
is invariant by a hyperspecial subgroup
K_{q}
of
\spin(V_{\ell})(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
, it is not equal to
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{sph}}
. To get around this, we give a criterion on
K_{q}
-invariant functions
\varphi_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{Z})
under which we can prove:

(0.9)

\beta_{\pi}(Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{sph}}))\neq 0\pmod{\mathfrak{p}^{n}}\implies\exists\,T^{\prime}\text{ s.t. }\beta_{\pi}(Z(T^{\prime},\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}))\neq 0\pmod{\mathfrak{p}^{n}}.

The function
\varphi_{q}^{{\operatorname{unr}}}
satisfies the criterion, so indeed we can use (
0.7
), (
0.8
), and (
0.9
) to obtain the local ramification of some class
\Res_{q}\kappa_{n}(q,T^{\prime},\varphi,\alpha_{n})
.
The lack of control over
T^{\prime}
(in particular its possible dependence on
q
) is the reason that we must consider the full family of classes
\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi,\alpha_{n})
.

The proof of (
0.9
) uses that the theta lift map

\Theta_{-}(\beta_{\pi}):\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})\to M_{5/2,2}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}O

factors as a product of local maps, defined purely in terms of the local Weil representation. Thus (
0.9
) can be reduced to a local question about the mod-
p
Weil representation of
\MP_{4}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\SO(V_{\ell})(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
on
\mathcal{S}(V_{\ell}^{2},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.

0.3.2.
Level-raising

In the discussion above, we asserted the existence of a level raising map

(0.10)

\alpha_{n}:H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to T_{\pi,n}

such that the classes
\kappa_{n}(q,T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}},\alpha_{n})
satisfy the identity (
0.8
). We now explain the construction of
\alpha_{n}
in more detail.
The weight spectral sequence for
\Sh(V_{q\ell})
gives rise to a diagram of the following form:

(0.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{-1}H^{1}\left(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}},H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Shimura(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\left(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}},H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Shimura(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)^{{\operatorname{Frob}}_{q}=1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O_{\mathfrak{p}}\left[\Shimura(V_{\ell})\right]_{\mathfrak{m}}/(\mathtt{T}^{\operatorname{lr}}_{q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}\simeq H^{1}(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}},T_{\pi,n})^{\Frob_{q}=1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n,\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\pi}}

where
\mathtt{T}^{\operatorname{lr}}_{q}
is a certain spherical Hecke operator measuring the level-raising congruence at the prime
q
;
M_{-1}
refers to the monodromy filtration; and the equality in the bottom row uses the level-raising condition on
q
. Any level-raising map as in (
0.10
) induces a map

\alpha_{n,\ast}
as in (
0.11
), not necessarily making the diagram commute.

Using an idea of Scholze
[
99
]
on “typic-ness” of Galois modules, we show that the data of a Hecke-equivariant
\alpha_{n,\ast}
is actually
equivalent
to the data of
\alpha_{n}
. Moreover, the identity
(
0.8
) is more or less equivalent to the commutativity of the diagram (
0.11
). So our task is to find the Hecke-equivariant dashed arrow in (
0.11
) lifting
\beta_{\pi}\circ\xi
.

One approach to finding the lift in (
0.11
) would be Taylor-Wiles patching, in the spirit of
[
66
]
.
This would show that
H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is free as a Hecke module, and as a byproduct that
the top arrow in (
0.11
) is an isomorphism.
However, the drawback of the patching argument is that it requires some strong “residual ramification” conditions on
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}}
, for primes
\ell|N(\pi)
; in general, one expects that the Hecke-module structure of
H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
may be more complicated.

Instead of taking this route, our method still uses Galois deformation theory, but now only to obtain a rather coarse quantitative control on the possible congruences between
\beta_{\pi}\circ\xi
and other Hecke eigensystems in
H^{1}(I_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh(V_{q\ell})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}})
. Indeed, if there were no congruences at all, then (
0.11
) would just be a diagram of
O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
-modules, and the lift
\alpha_{n,\ast}
would exist for trivial reasons. In general, we are able to lift
\beta_{\pi}\circ\xi
only after multiplying by a generator of
\mathfrak{p}^{C}
, where
C
is essentially the length of a certain adjoint Selmer group for
T_{\pi,n}
.
We can control
C
using a theorem of Newton and Thorne
[
84
,
111
]
that
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},\ad^{0}\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})=0;
since
C
is independent of
n
and
q
(and actually 0 for cofinitely many
\mathfrak{p}
) its appearance causes no harm in the argument.
3
3

3

We remark that the theorem of Newton and Thorne still uses Taylor-Wiles patching, but in a more flexible context.

0.4.
Sketch of the proof in the rank one case

Let
V
be the quadratic space from §
0.1
. For an auxiliary prime
q_{1}
satisfying a level-raising congruence condition, we consider the “nearby” quadratic space
V_{q_{1}}
, which has trivial discriminant and signature
(5,0)
, and is ramified precisely at
q_{1}
and the primes of ramification for
V
.

The key point to prove Theorem
C
is to produce a Hecke-equivariant map of the form

(0.12)

\beta_{q_{1}}:\Sh(V_{q_{1}})\to O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}\;\text{ s.t. }\;\exists\,T,\varphi\text{ with }\beta_{q_{1}}(Z(T,\varphi))\neq 0,

where
\varphi
lies in
\mathcal{S}(V_{q_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})
,
\Sh(V_{q_{1}})
is a finite double coset space like the one in (
0.5
), the Hecke module structure on
O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
is given by
\pi
, and again the choice of level structure is suppressed for simplicity. Once we have
\beta_{q_{1}}
, a similar argument to the proof of Theorem
A
allows us to produce a Galois cohomology class

\kappa_{n}(q_{1}q_{2})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})
which is ramified at
q_{2}
, crystalline at
p
, and unramified at
\ell
for
\ell\nmid N(\pi)pq_{1}q_{2}
. This class is then used as the input to the duality argument to bound
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})
.

Let
Z(T,\varphi)\in\CH^{2}(\Sh(V))
be the class from Theorem
C
with
\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\Pi_{f}}(Z(T,\varphi))\neq 0
.
The idea to find
\beta_{q_{1}}
is to study the special fiber of
\Sh(V)
at
q_{1}
, a prime of good reduction. The supersingular locus is purely one-dimensional, indexed by a Shimura set
\Sh(V_{q_{1}})
.
We have the Abel-Jacobi map on the special fiber:

(0.13)

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}:\CH^{2}(\Sh(V)_{\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}}})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}},H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Sh(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{1}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}})=H^{1}_{\operatorname{unr}}(\mathbb{Q}_{q_{1}},H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Sh(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}).

Restricting (
0.13
) to supersingular cycles gives a map

(0.14)

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m},{ss}}:\mathbb{Z}[\Sh(V_{q_{1}})]\to H^{1}_{\operatorname{unr}}(\mathbb{Q}_{q_{1}},H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Sh(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}).

Composing with a map
H^{3}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\Sh(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O_{\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
, cf. (
0.1
), and noting that
H^{1}_{\operatorname{unr}}(\mathbb{Q}_{q_{1}},T_{\pi,n})
is free of rank one over
O/\mathfrak{p}^{n}
when
q_{1}
satisfies the level-raising congruence condition modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
, we obtain our
\beta_{q_{1}}
.

Using the Chebotarev density theorem and the local-global compatibility of the Abel-Jacobi map, one can ensure
\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}}})
is nonzero for a good choice of
q_{1}
. Now, if the special fiber
Z(T,\varphi)_{\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}}}
were purely supersingular, then we could identify this local Abel-Jacobi class with the image of a special cycle in
\mathbb{Z}[\Sh(V_{q_{1}})]
under
\beta_{q_{1}}
, and obtain the nonvanishing (
0.12
).
(This is the strategy of
[
7
]
, where the role of
Z(T,\varphi)
is played by a Heegner point.) Unfortunately, such is not the case for general
Z(T,\varphi)
.
4
4

4

One could imagine modifying
\varphi_{q}
to ensure a purely supersingular special fiber, and applying a version of (
0.9
) to ensure that
Z(T,\varphi)
remains nontrivial under this change of test function. Somewhat to our surprise, this strategy fails: any appropriate choice of
\varphi_{q}
fails the criterion under which we prove (
0.9
).

Instead, we use an auxiliary
\spin_{4}
Shimura variety
S
such that
Z(T,\varphi)\subset S\subset\Sh(V)
. On the special fiber of
S
, the supersingular locus is one-dimensional, and – by
[
112
]
– any one-cycle is cohomologically equivalent to a linear combination of supersingular cycles, at least after the application of a certain
\spin_{4}
-Hecke operator
\mathtt{T}_{q_{1}}
. We leverage this to rewrite
\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(\mathtt{T}_{q_{1}}\cdot Z(T,\varphi)_{\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}}})
as the image of some
Z(T,\varphi^{\prime})
under
\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m},{ss}}
, with
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{q_{1}}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},\mathbb{Z})
.

Now it remains to choose
q_{1}
so that
\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(\mathtt{T}_{q_{1}}\cdot Z(T,\varphi)_{\mathbb{F}_{q_{1}}})
is nontrivial.
For this choice to be possible, we need some non-entanglement between
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
and the Galois representations appearing in the cohomology of
S
, which are closely related to Hilbert modular forms. In particular, the support of
Z(T,\varphi)
in the cohomology of
S
needs to avoid some problematic Hecke eigensystems associated to CM forms.
To ensure this, we use a version of the modifying-the-test-function trick from (
0.9
), this time at (non-level-raising) auxiliary primes
\ell\neq q_{1},q_{2}
. Since the trick uses representation theory, it relies on the modularity conjecture recalled after the statement of Theorem
C
. (The subtleties that appear in this part of the argument prevent us from bounding the dual Selmer group
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}/T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}})
in the context of Theorem
C
, in contrast to the rank zero case.)

One final difficulty that arises in the proof of Theorem
C
is the application of the level-raising arguments in §
0.3.2
to
\beta_{q_{1}}
. Since
\beta_{q_{1}}
does not lift to a characteristic-zero Hecke eigenfunction in general, we cannot directly apply the theorem of Newton and Thorne to produce the lift in the diagram (
0.11
). Instead, we use the relative deformation theory developed by Fakhruddin, Khare, and Patrikis, along with a certain control of level-raised adjoint Selmer groups, to show that either
\beta_{q_{1}}
lifts to an eigenfunction, or the Hecke eigensystem of
\pi
is congruent modulo
\mathfrak{p}^{n}
to that of an automorphic representation
\pi^{\prime}
ramified at
q_{1}

and

q_{2}
; in either case, we leverage the congruence to solve the lifting problem in (
0.11
). It is at this step that the extra hypothesis in Theorem
C
(that
\pi
does not arise from certain automorphic inductions) enters the argument; if the image of the Galois representation
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is too small, then we are unable to control the adjoint Selmer groups needed to apply Fakhruddin, Khare, and Patrikis’ method.

0.5.
Comparison to other work

When
\pi_{p}
is Borel-ordinary with respect to
\mathfrak{p}
, and under a slightly different large-image condition, Theorem
A
can be deduced from the main result of
[
72
]
, which also covers more general weights. However, the existence of ordinary primes is still an open problem for general automorphic representations of
\GSP_{4}
, so the ordinarity hypothesis is potentially a serious one.

As indicated in §
0.2
, this article fits into an extensive literature of bounding Selmer groups using level-raising congruences (the method of “bipartite Euler systems”). Compared to these works, one of the main underlying difficulties to prove Theorem
A
is the non-factorizability of
\spin_{3}-\spin_{5}
period integrals, which is ultimately responsible for the appearance of the exotic test function
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{unr}}
(rather than the indictor function of a self-dual lattice or a translate of such by the spherical Hecke algebra) in the identity (
0.8
). Another way to formulate this obstruction is in terms of spherical functions and Hironaka’s conjecture
[
46
,
26
]
. To our knowledge, the only prior work on bipartite Euler systems facing this challenge was the recently appeared PhD thesis of Corato Zanarella
[
28
]
. Interestingly, rather than our change-of-test-functions technique, the issue in
op. cit.
is resolved using derived algebraic geometry; in our context, the analogous idea would be to work with
Z(T,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}})
for more general
\varphi_{q}^{\operatorname{ram}}
, even in the absence of a good integral model.

Another novel aspect in the present work is in finding the
\beta_{q_{1}}
in (
0.12
). To our knowledge, the idea of using intersection theory on the special fiber of an auxiliary Shimura variety
S
, in which the supersingular locus and the special cycle are of complementary dimension, is a new one.

Finally, we warn the reader that the “first and second reciprocity laws” (corresponding to finding
\kappa_{n}(q)
and
\beta_{q_{1}}
, respectively, in the discussions above) in the table of contents are named only by analogy to
[
7
]
. In both cases, the full reciprocity law would constitute an equality, whereas we only prove an inequality, and that only up to a bounded error. The exact statements are Theorems
8.5.1
and
11.2.6
.

0.6.
Comments on the endoscopic case

An automorphic representation
\pi
as in the beginning of §
0.1
is called
endoscopic
associated to a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
of
cuspidal automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
– necessarily arising from classical modular forms of weights 2 and 4, respectively, and trivial nebentypus characters –
if the associated Galois representations satisfy

(0.15)

\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}=\rho_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}\boxplus\rho_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}

for one, or equivalently all, primes
\mathfrak{p}
of
E
. Here we normalize
\rho_{\pi_{i},\mathfrak{p}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{p}})
to have cyclotomic determinant; also let
V_{\pi_{i},\mathfrak{p}}
be the underlying Galois module. The set of
\pi
satisfying (
0.15
) is by definition the endoscopic
L
-packet
\Pi(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
. If
V
is a quadratic space of signature
(3,2)
and
\Pi=\Pi_{f}\otimes\Pi_{\infty}
is an automorphic representation of
\spin(V)(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
nearly equivalent to the members of
\Pi(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, then we have

(0.16)

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{\mathfrak{p}})[\Pi_{f}]=\begin{cases}\Pi_{f}^{K}\otimes V_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}(-2),&i=3,\,\Pi_{\infty}\text{ generic},\\
\Pi_{f}^{K}\otimes V_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}(-2),&i=3,\,\Pi_{\infty}\text{ holomorphic},\\
0&i\neq 3.\end{cases}

Note here that
\Pi_{\infty}
is uniquely determined by
\Pi_{f}
via the Arthur multiplicity formula, which is known in this case
[
21
]
.
In particular, we still have a well-defined map

(0.17)

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\Pi_{f}}:\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))\to\Pi_{f}^{K}\otimes\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}\oplus V_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}})\right).

The analogue of Theorem
C
is now:

Proposition D

(Theorem
12.3.3
)
.

Suppose
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are non-CM, and
\mathfrak{p}|p
is a prime of
E
such that:

(1)

\pi_{1,p}
and
\pi_{2,p}
are unramified.

(2)

The residual representations
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}
are both absolutely irreducible, and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}\oplus\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}
is generic (Definition
2.7.3
).

(3)

For both
i=1,2
, there exists a prime
q\nmid N(\pi)
such that
q^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
,
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{i},\mathfrak{p}}(\Frob_{q})
has eigenvalues
\left\{1,q\right\}
, and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{3-i},\mathfrak{p}}(\Frob_{q})
has eigenvalues
\left\{\alpha,q/\alpha\right\}
with
\alpha\not\in\left\{\pm 1,\pm q,q^{2},q^{-1}\right\}.

Suppose as well that

(
\ast
)

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}\otimes V_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}(-1))=0.

Then for any Kudla cycle
Z(T,\varphi)\in\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V))
,

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\Pi_{f}}(Z(T,\varphi))\neq 0\implies\dim_{E_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}})+\dim_{E_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},V_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}})=1.

Remarks
.

(i)

The conditions (
1
)-(
3
) hold for cofinitely many
\mathfrak{p}
(Lemma
4.1.5
and Proposition
C.4.11
).

(ii)

The condition
(\ast)
is always expected to hold, by a classical result of Shahidi on nonvanishing of Rankin-Selberg
L
-values
[
101
]
. Unfortunately, we remark that
(\ast)
does
not
follow from the main result of
[
61
]
, because the necessary large-image condition is never satisfied for
V_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}\otimes V_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}(-1)
. The role of
(\ast)
in the proof is to control congruences to non-endoscopic automorphic representations of
\spin_{5}
groups.

(iii)

Consider the case when
V
is split, so that
\Sh_{K}(V)
is a classical Siegel threefold, and
\pi_{1}
is associated to an elliptic curve
E
over
\mathbb{Q}
.
In particular, the Abel-Jacobi map (
0.17
) gives a way to construct classes in
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},T_{p}E)
from special cycles on Siegel threefolds. Weissauer asked in
[
125
]

how such classes related to the classical theory of Heegner points, a question to which Proposition

D
provides a partial answer: like the Selmer classes coming from Heegner points, these Abel-Jacobi classes can be nonzero only if
E
has rank one (at least modulo the assumption on Rankin-Selberg convolutions).

(iv)

In the text, we prove a stronger version of Proposition
D
that includes some CM cases.

Similarly, the methods used to Prove Theorem
A
can also be used for endoscopic representations under the condition
(\ast)
. This case is included in Theorem
9.2.4
for completeness, but note that the result gives nothing new beyond Kato’s work
[
49
]
.

0.7.
Organization of the paper

In §
1
, we lay out the notational conventions for the article and cover preliminary materials on involutions, Clifford algebras, and Selmer groups. In §
2
, we collect the necessary background results related to automorphic representations, Galois representations, and Shimura varieties. The most important role of this section is to make some of the results of
[
97
]
(on global Jacquet-Langlands transfers for inner forms of
\GSP_{4}
) unconditional on Conjecture 7.5 of
op. cit.
In §
3
, we define Kudla’s cycles
Z(T,\varphi)
and their analogues on compact
\spin
groups, and explain their relation to classical theta lifts. In §
4
, we define the Galois cohomology classes and special periods used in the Euler system arguments. In §
5
, we use the mod-
p
theory of the Weil representation to prove the change-of-test-functions criterion explained above in (
0.9
); this is one of the most crucial (and technical) parts of the argument.

In §
6
, we turn to geometry, studying a ramified Rapoport-Zink space for
\spin_{5}
. This section is based on
[
119
]
, although we need more details on intersection theory for our applications to special cycles.
In §
7
, these results are applied to study the special fiber of the ramified
\spin_{5}
Shimura variety, and compute the local part of the Abel-Jacobi image of special cycles. (Some of the results in this section are generalizations of those in
[
120
]
.) In §
8
, we perform the level-raising and complete the program described in §
0.3
above. In §
9
, we prove the main results in the rank zero case. In §
10
, we study special cycles on the special fibers of
\spin_{4}
and
\spin_{5}
Shimura varieties with good reduction. In §
11
and §
12
, we prove our main result in the rank one case.

The paper contains three appendices: in the short Appendix
A
, we explain the relation of
the cohomology of
\spin_{4}
Shimura varieties to Hilbert modular forms, which is well-known but for which we were unable to find a suitable reference. In Appendix
B
, we develop a general framework for deformation-theoretic characteristic-zero level raising of
G
-valued Galois representations, using ideas from
[
30
]
.
These results are most important for the proof of the rank one case. In Appendix
C
, we prove some large-image results for the
p
-adic Galois representations that appear in the article, which are necessary for various Chebotarev arguments.

0.8.

I wish to thank my PhD advisor, Mark Kisin, for his encouragement and advice while I worked on this project.
I am also grateful to Aaron Landesman, Alice Lin, Sam Mundy, Chris Skinner, Shiang Tang, Salim Tayou, Wei Zhang, and especially Murilo Corato Zanarella for helpful conversations. Thank you to Karen Schlain for help with typesetting. This work was supported by NSF grants DGE-1745303 and DMS-2401823.

1.
Preliminaries

1.1.
Notation

1.1.1.
Number fields and Galois groups

Let
L\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
be a number field.

•

We denote by
O_{L}
the ring of integers. If
\mathfrak{p}\subset O_{L}
is a prime, then we write
O_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
L_{\mathfrak{p}}
for the respective completions, and
\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}
for a uniformizer of
O_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

•

Let
G_{L}\coloneqq\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/L)
be the absolute Galois group. If
S
is a finite set of places of
L
, we denote by
L^{S}
the maximal unramified-outside-
S
extension of
L
, and set
G_{L,S}\coloneqq\Gal(L^{S}/L)\subset G_{L}
. If
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime of
L
, we also write
G_{L_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for the absolute Galois group of
L_{\mathfrak{p}}
, with inertia subgroup
I_{L_{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset G_{L_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

•

For any
p
,
\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}:G_{L}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
denotes the
p
-adic cyclotomic character. We normalize the definition of Hodge-Tate weights so that
\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
has weight one.

1.1.2.
Adèle groups and class field theory

•

For a number field
K
, let
\mathbb{A}_{K}
be the adèle ring; when
K=\mathbb{Q}
we typically write
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. For a finite set
S
of places of
\mathbb{Q}
, we write

\mathbb{A}_{S}=\prod_{v\in S}\mathbb{Q}_{v},\;\;\mathbb{A}^{S}=\prod_{v\not\in S}^{\prime}\mathbb{Q}_{v},\;\;\mathbb{A}_{f}^{S}=\prod_{v\not\in S\cup\left\{\infty\right\}}^{\prime}\mathbb{Q}_{v}.

•

If
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
is an irreducible admissible representation of
G(\mathbb{A})
for some
\mathbb{Q}
-group
G
, then for a squarefree integer
D
,
\pi_{f}^{D}
denotes
\otimes^{\prime}_{\ell\nmid D}\pi_{\ell}
, and
\pi_{D}
denotes
\otimes_{\ell|D}\pi_{\ell}
. The similar notations
\pi_{f}^{S}
,
\pi_{S}
hold for finite sets of primes
S
.

•

For any prime
p
, let
\langle p\rangle\in\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}
be the image of
p
under the natural inclusion
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}
.

•

We always normalize the reciprocity maps of class field theory
to send uniformizers to geometric Frobenius.

•

For any number field
L
and finite-order character
\chi:L^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{L}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
, with
k
a field, we write
\rec(\chi):G_{L}\to k^{\times}
for the pullback by the reciprocity map.

1.1.3.
Coefficient rings

For coefficient rings, we will often use a discrete valuation ring
O
which is a finite flat extension of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, with uniformizer
\varpi
. In this context:

•

We denote by
\CNL_{O}
the category of complete local Noetherian
O
-algebras with residue field
O/\varpi
.

•

For any torsion
O
-module
M
and any
m\in M
,
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\varpi}(m)\geq 0
denotes the least integer such that
\varpi^{{\operatorname{ord}}_{\varpi}(m)}m=0.

1.1.4.
Symplectic groups

•

For any
n
, let
\Omega_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{n}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&\mathbf{0}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2n}(\mathbb{Z})
,
where
\mathbf{0}_{n}
is the
n\times n
matrix of zeros and
I_{n}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix.

•

Define the algebraic group
\GSP_{2n}
over
\mathbb{Z}
by

(1.1)

\GSP_{2n}(R)=\left\{(g,\lambda)\in\GL_{2n}(R)\times R^{\times}\,:\,g\Omega g^{t}=\lambda\Omega\right\}

for any ring
R
.

•

We have the natural similitude character
\nu:\GSP_{2n}\to\mathbb{G}_{m},
and the symplectic group
\SP_{2n}\subset\GSP_{2n}
is the kernel of
\nu
.

•

For any prime
p
, we write
\langle p\rangle\in\GSP_{2n}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
for the scalar matrix corresponding to
\langle p\rangle\in\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}
.

1.1.5.
GSpin groups

Let
V
be a quadratic space over a field
F
.

•

We denote the pairing
V\times V\to F
by
(v,w)\mapsto v\cdot w
.

•

The Clifford algebra of
V
is the associative
F
-algebra
C(V)
generated by
v\in V
, subject to the relation

v^{2}=(v\cdot v)1.

There is a natural
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
grading on
C(V)
, with respect to which the plus part is denoted
C^{+}(V)
.

•

We denote by
\ast
the natural involution on
C(V)
, determined by
v^{\ast}=v
. Then
\spin(V)
is the algebraic group over
F
defined by

\spin(V)(R)=\left\{(g,\lambda)\in\left(C^{+}(V)\otimes R\right)^{\times}\times R^{\times}\,:\,gg^{\ast}=\lambda\right\}.

The natural similitude character is again denoted
\nu
.

•

If
F=\mathbb{Q}
, then for any prime
p
we again denote by
\langle p\rangle\in\spin(V)(\mathbb{A}_{f})
the scalar element
p\in C^{+}(V)^{\times}.

1.1.6.
Quaternion algebras and quadratic spaces

•

For a squarefree integer
D\geq 1
, let
B_{D}
be the quaternion algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
which ramifies at the factors of
D
, and possibly at infinity. Let
\ast_{D}
be the standard involution on
B_{D}
. We also denote by
\ast_{D}
the involution on
M_{n}(B_{D})
given by the composite of
\ast_{D}
and transposition.

•

For all squarefree
D
, let

(1.2)

V_{D}\coloneqq M_{2}(B_{D})^{\ast_{D}=1,\tr=0},

which is a 5-dimensional quadratic space of trivial discriminant, whose Hasse invariant coincides with that of
B_{D}
. The signature of
V_{D}
is
(5,0)
or
(3,2)
when
B_{D}
is ramified or split at infinity, respectively.

•

We note that
\spin(V_{D})
is an inner form of
\spin(V_{1})\cong\GSP_{4}
.

1.1.7.
Algebraic geometry

•

For a closed subscheme
X
of a scheme
Y
, we denote by
\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}
the normal sheaf.

•

If
X
is a scheme,
X_{\operatorname{red}}\subset X
denotes the maximal reduced subscheme.

1.1.8.
Miscellaneous

•

For a squarefree
integer
D\geq 1
, we denote by
\div(D)
its set of prime factors, and let
\sigma(D)\coloneqq\#\div(D)
.

•

Suppose
V
is an
F
-vector space for a nonarchimedean local field
F
with ring of integers
O_{F}
. For two
O_{F}
-lattices
\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}
of
V
, the notation
\Lambda\subset_{n}\Lambda^{\prime}
means that
\Lambda^{\prime}/\Lambda
has
O_{F}
-length
n
.

•

For any group
G
, we let
Z_{G}\subset G
denote the center.

•

For a prime
p
, let
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
be the Witt vectors of
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

1.2.
Central simple algebras and involutions

1.2.1.

Let
M
be a central simple algebra over a field
F
of characteristic zero. Throughout this paper an involution on
M
will be understood to mean an involution of the first kind, i.e. fixing
F
. Such involutions fall into two categories: main type or nebentype (also called symplectic and orthogonal type
[
52
]
). For example, the transpose involution on
M=M_{2}(F)
is of nebentype; the standard involution on a quaternion algebra is of main type.
For later use, we now recall some basic facts.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

Let
(M_{1},\ast_{1})
and
(M_{2},\ast_{2})
be two central simple algebras equipped with involutions.
The induced involution
\ast_{1}\otimes\ast_{2}
on
M
is of nebentype if and only if
\ast_{1}
and
\ast_{2}
are of the
same
type.

Proof.

This is
[
52
, Proposition 2.23]
.
∎

1.2.3.

Suppose
F=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, and recall that an element
\mu\in M
is called
totally positive
if it has strictly positive eigenvalues as an endomorphism of
M
.

Proposition 1.2.4
.

Let
M=M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
or
M_{n}(\mathbb{H})
, and suppose
M
is equipped with a
positive
involution
\ast
, i.e. such that
\tr(a^{\ast}a)>0
for all
0\neq a\in M
. Then the positive involutions on
M
are those of the form

a\mapsto\mu a^{\ast}\mu^{-1},

where
\mu^{\ast}=\mu
and either
\mu
or
-\mu
is totally positive.
In particular, all positive involutions on
M
have the same type.

Proof.

This is
[
116
, Proposition 8.4.7 and Lemma 8.4.12]
.
∎

1.2.5.

The tensor product of two quaternion algebras is called a
biquaternion algebra
(BQA). Involutions of nebentype on rational BQAs are particularly simple:

Lemma 1.2.6
.

Let
M
be a BQA over
\mathbb{Q}
, and let
\ast_{1},

\ast_{2}
be two involutions of
M
of main type. If either
M\otimes\mathbb{R}
is split, or
\ast_{1},

\ast_{2}
are both positive, then there exists an element
g\in M^{\times}

such that

\inner(g)\circ\ast_{1}=\ast_{2}\circ\inner(g).

In particular, conjugation by
g
defines an isomorphism
(M,\ast_{1})\simeq(M,\ast_{2})
.

Here,
\inner(g)
is the automorphism
a\mapsto gag^{-1}
of
M
.

Proof.

By the Skolem-Noether theorem, there exists
h\in M^{\times}

such that
\inner(h)\circ\ast_{1}=\ast_{2}.

The condition on
g

in the lemma is equivalent to
h=\lambda g^{\ast_{1}}g
, for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
.
By
[
52
, Theorem 16.19]
, there exists such a
g

if and only if the Pfaffian norm of
h
(with respect to
\ast_{1}
)
belongs to
(\mathbb{Q}^{\times})^{2}\nerd(M^{\times})
. If
M\otimes\mathbb{R}

is split, then
\nerd(M^{\times})=\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, so we are done. If
M\otimes\mathbb{R}

is not split, then
\nerd(M^{\times})=\mathbb{Q}^{\times}_{>0}
, and it suffices to show that the equation
h=\lambda g^{\ast_{1}}g

has a solution over
\mathbb{R}
. Assuming without loss of generality that
\ast_{1}
is the standard involution on
M\otimes\mathbb{R}\simeq M_{2}(\mathbb{H})
, this can be checked directly using Proposition
1.2.4
.
∎

1.2.7.

Let
F
be a nonarchimedean local field with ring of integers
O_{F}
, and let
B
be the unique nonsplit quaternion algebra over
O_{F}
. We denote by
O_{B}
the unique maximal
O_{F}
-order in
B
, with uniformizer
\pi\in O_{B}
and natural valuation
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\pi}
.

Proposition 1.2.8
.

Suppose
g\in\GL_{n}(B)

satisfies

gM_{n}(O_{B})g^{-1}=M_{n}(O_{B}).

Then, up to rescaling by an element of
F^{\times}
, we have either
g\in\GL_{n}(O_{B})

or
g\in\pi\GL_{n}(O_{B})
.

Proof.

Suppose
g

is given by the matrix
(g_{ij})

and
g^{-1}

is given by the matrix
(h_{kl})
. Let
\alpha_{jk}

be the matrix with a 1 in the
jk

position and 0s elsewhere; since
g\alpha_{jk}g^{-1}\in M_{n}(O_{B})
, we see that

(1.3)

g_{ij}h_{kl}\in O_{B},\;\;\text{for all}\,i,j,k,l.

Without loss of generality, by rescaling
g
, we may assume
g_{ij}\in O_{B}

for all
i,j

but
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\pi}(g_{ij})\leq 1

for some
i,j
. If
h_{ij}\in O_{B}

for all
i,j
, then
g\in\GL_{n}(O_{B})
, so suppose without loss of generality that
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\pi}(h_{kl})<0

for some
k,l
.
It follows from (
1.3
) that
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\pi}(g_{ij})\geq 1

for all
i,j
, with equality holding for some
i,j
, and
{\operatorname{ord}}_{\pi}(h_{kl})\geq-1

for all
k,l
; in particular,
g\in\pi\GL_{n}(O_{B})
.
∎

Motivated by Proposition
1.2.8
, we make the following definition.

Definition 1.2.9
.

An involution
\ast

of
M_{n}(B)

stabilizing
M_{n}(O_{B})

is called of

unit type
if it is of the form
\alpha^{\ast}=g\overline{\alpha}^{t}g^{-1}

for some
g\in\GL_{n}(O_{B})
, and of

non-unit type
if it is of the form
\alpha^{\ast}=g\overline{\alpha}^{t}g^{-1}

for some
g\in\pi\GL_{n}(O_{B})
.

Similarly, if
B

is a quaternion algebra over
\mathbb{Q}

and
p

is a prime such that
B\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}

is not split, let
O_{B}\subset B

be the unique maximal
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-order. Then an involution
\ast

of
M_{n}(B)

stabilizing
M_{n}(O_{B})
is called of unit or non-unit type according to whether the induced involution on
M_{n}(B)\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}

is of unit or non-unit type.

Remark 1.2.10
.

By Proposition
1.2.8
, an involution
\ast

of
M_{n}(B)

stabilizing
M_{n}(O_{B})

induces an involution on
M_{n}(O_{B}/\pi)
, which acts trivially on the center of
M_{n}(O_{B}/\pi)

if and only if
\ast

is of non-unit type.

1.2.11.

The same proof as Proposition
1.2.8
also shows:

Proposition 1.2.12
.

Let
F
be a nonarchimedean local field with ring of integers
O_{F}
.
If
g\in\GL_{n}(F)
satisfies

gM_{n}(O_{F})g^{-1}=M_{n}(O_{F}),

then we have
g\in F^{\times}\GL_{n}(O_{F})
.

1.3.
PEL data

1.3.1.

Recall the notion of a PEL datum
\mathcal{D}=(B,\ast,V,\psi)
from
[
78
, Chapter 8]
, and set
C=\End_{B}(V)
. Then
C
is equipped with an involution
c\mapsto c^{\prime}
, the adjoint with respect to
\psi
, and the
\mathbb{Q}
-group
G_{\mathcal{D}}

associated to
\mathcal{D}

is defined by

G_{\mathcal{D}}(R)=\left\{(g,\lambda)\in(C\otimes R)^{\times}\times R^{\times}\,:\,gg^{\prime}=\lambda\right\}.

To
\mathcal{D}
, there is associated the reflex field
E=E_{\mathcal{D}}
, and, for any compact open subgroup
K\subset G_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, a moduli functor
M_{K}
over
E
. Let us briefly recall the definition of
M_{K}
, for which more details can be found in
[
53
, p. 390]
.
For a connected scheme
S\to\Spec E
,
M_{K}(S)

is the set of isomorphism classes of tuples
(A,\iota,\lambda,\eta)
where:

•

A/S
is an abelian scheme up to isogeny;

•

\iota:B\hookrightarrow\End^{0}(A/S)
is an embedding satisfying the Kottwitz determinant condition derived from
\mathcal{D}
;

•

\lambda:A\to A^{\vee}

is a quasi-polarization such that
\iota(b^{\ast})^{\vee}\circ\lambda=\lambda\circ\iota(b)

for all
b\in B
;

•

\eta
is a
K
-level structure for
A
, i.e., for any geometric point
s
of
S
, a
\pi_{1}(S,s)
-stable
K
-orbit of isomorphisms

\eta:H_{1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(A_{s},\mathbb{A}_{f})\xrightarrow{\sim}V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{A}_{f}

respecting the actions of
B
and the symplectic pairings on both sides up to a scalar.

If
K
is neat, then
M_{K}
is represented by a smooth quasi-projective scheme over
E
.

1.3.2.

Let
p
be a prime. A self-dual
p
-integral refinement
\mathscr{D}

of
\mathcal{D}

is the additional data of a
\ast
-stable maximal
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-order
O_{B}\subset B

and a self-dual
O_{B}
-stable
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-lattice
\Lambda\subset V
. (This is a special case of the notion in
[
94
, §6]
.)
For a compact open subgroup
K^{p}\subset G_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
, the corresponding moduli problem
\mathcal{M}_{K^{p}}
is defined as follows. For a connected scheme
S\to\Spec\mathcal{O}_{E}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{K^{p}}(S)
is the set of isomorphism classes of tuples
(A,\iota,\lambda,\eta^{p})
where:

•

A/S
is an abelian scheme up to prime-to-
p
isogeny;

•

\iota:O_{B}\hookrightarrow\And(A/S)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}

is an embedding satisfying the Kottwitz condition;

•

\lambda:A\rightarrow A^{\vee}
is a prime-to-
p
quasi-polarization such that
\iota(b^{\ast})^{\vee}\circ\lambda=\lambda\circ\iota(b)

for all
b\in O_{B}
;

•

\eta^{p}
is a
K^{p}
-level structure, i.e., for any geometric point
s
of
S
, a
\pi_{1}(S,s)
-stable
K^{p}
-orbit of isomorphisms

\eta:H_{1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(A_{s},\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})\xrightarrow{\sim}V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p}

respecting the actions of
B
and the symplectic pairings on both sides up to a scalar.

If
K^{p}
is neat, then
\mathcal{M}_{K^{p}}

is represented by a quasi-projective scheme over
O_{E}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
.
Its generic fiber is
M_{K^{p}K_{p}}
, where
K_{p}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\Lambda)
.

Lemma 1.3.3
.

Let
S
be a scheme and
B
a simple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with involution
\ast
of the first kind. Suppose given an abelian scheme
A/S
with an embedding of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras

\iota:B\hookrightarrow\End^{0}(A/S),

and a quasi-polarization
\lambda:A\to A^{\vee}
such that

\iota(b^{\ast})^{\vee}\circ\lambda=\lambda\circ\iota(b),\;\;\forall b\in B.

Then for any totally positive
g\in B^{\times}
with
g^{\ast}=g
,
\lambda\circ g
defines a quasi-polarization of
A
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma when
S=\Spec k
with
k
an algebraically closed field. In this case, by the discussion in
[
79
, Chapter 21, Application III]
, it suffices to show that
g
has strictly positive eigenvalues on
\End^{0}(A)
. But this follows from the positivity of the eigenvalues on
B
, because
\End^{0}(A)
is a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra containing
B
, and any such is a product of copies of
B
as a
B
-module.
∎

The following
corollary is immediate.

Corollary 1.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(B,\ast,V,\psi)

be a PEL datum, where
B
is a simple
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra and
\ast
is an involution of the first kind. Suppose given a totally positive
g\in B^{\times}

such that
g^{\ast}=g
, and let
\mathcal{D}_{g}=(B,\ast_{g},V,\psi_{g}),

where
\ast_{g}\coloneqq g\circ\ast\circ g^{-1}
and

\psi_{g}(x,y)\coloneqq\psi(x,g^{-1}y),\;\;\forall x,y\in V.

Then
G_{\mathcal{D}}=G_{\mathcal{D}_{g}}
, and, for all
K\subset G_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there is a canonical isomorphism

M_{\mathcal{D},K}\xrightarrow{\sim}M_{\mathcal{D}_{g},K}

defined by

(A,\iota,\lambda,\eta)\mapsto(A,\iota,\lambda\circ g,\eta).

If
\mathscr{D}=(O_{B},\ast,\Lambda,\psi)

is a self-dual
p
-integral refinement of
\mathcal{D}

and
g
lies in
O_{B}^{\times}
, then
\mathscr{D}_{g}=(O_{B},\ast_{g},\Lambda,\psi_{g})

is a self-dual
p
-integral refinement of
\mathcal{D}_{g}
and the above isomorphism extends to an isomorphism of integral models

\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{D},K^{p}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{D}_{g},K^{p}}

for all compact open
K^{p}\subset G_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{p})
.

∎

We also deduce:

Corollary 1.3.5
.

Let
B
be an indefinite quaternion algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
, with
O_{B}\subset B
a maximal
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-order, and
fix an integer
n\geq 1
.
Let
\ast
be a
positive involution on
M_{n}(O_{B})
, of non-unit type if
B\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is ramified. Then there exists an abelian scheme
A
over
\Spec\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
of dimension
2n
with supersingular reduction, a
prime-to-
p
quasi-polarizon
\lambda:A\to A^{\vee}
, and an embedding
\iota:M_{n}(O_{B})\hookrightarrow\End(A)\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)},
such that

\iota(b^{\ast})^{\vee}\circ\lambda=\lambda\circ\iota(b),\;\;\forall b\in M_{n}(O_{B}).

Proof.

Using Lemma
1.3.3
along with Propositions
1.2.8
and
1.2.12
, we reduce to the case that
\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha^{\dagger t}
for all
\alpha\in M_{n}(O_{B})
, where
\dagger
is a positive involution on
O_{B}
, of non-unit type if
B
ramifies at
p
. Thus it suffices to prove the corollary when
n=1
, in which case it is well-known from the classical theory of Shimura curves; see the discussions in
[
12
, Chapter III]
.
∎

1.4.
PEL data for
\spin_{3,2}
groups

1.4.1.

In this article, we will consider PEL data that arise in the following way. Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree with
\sigma(D)

even
, and let
q
be a prime, possibly with
q|D
. Suppose fixed a maximal
\mathbb{Z}_{(q)}
-order
O_{D}\subset B_{D}
, with a nebentype involution
\ast
, and an embedding
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 1.4.2
.

(1)

An
(O_{D},\ast)
-triple is a triple
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0})
, where:

(a)

A_{0}
is an abelian scheme over
\Spec\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{q}
of rank 4 with supersingular reduction;

(b)

\iota_{0}
is an embedding
O_{D}\hookrightarrow\End(A_{0})\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(q)}
;

(c)

\lambda_{0}:A_{0}\to A_{0}^{\vee}
is a prime-to-
q
polarization, such that

\iota_{0}(\alpha^{\ast})^{\vee}\circ\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{0}\circ\iota_{0}(\alpha),\;\;\forall\alpha\in O_{D}.

(2)

Given an
(O_{D},\ast)
-triple as above, we set
H\coloneqq H_{1}(A_{0}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q}),
with its canonical symplectic form
\psi
. Let
\Lambda\subset H
be the lattice
H_{1}(A_{0}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Z}_{(q)})
. The PEL datum associated to
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0})
is defined by

\mathcal{D}=(B_{D},\ast,H,\psi),

with self-dual
q
-integral refinement

\mathscr{D}=(O_{D},\ast,\Lambda,\psi).

1.4.3.

Let
D\cdot q\coloneqq Dq/\operatorname{gcd}(D,q)
.
Given an
(O_{D},\ast)
-triple
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0})
:

•

H
is a
B_{D}
-module, and
\End(H,B_{D})
is isomorphic to
M_{2}(B_{D})
. The adjoint involution
\dagger
on
\End(H,B_{D})
is of main type (by Proposition
1.2.2
, because the adjoint involution on
\End(H)=B_{D}\otimes\End(H,B_{D})
is of main type).

•

Set
\overline{A}_{0}\coloneqq(A_{0})_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
, with its induced
\mathcal{O}_{D}
-action
\overline{\iota}_{0}
and polarization
\overline{\lambda}_{0}
. Then
\End^{0}(\overline{A}_{0},\overline{\iota}_{0})
is isomorphic to
M_{2}(B_{D\cdot q})
, and its Rosati involution
\dagger
is positive, hence of main type by Proposition
1.2.4
.

Definition 1.4.4
.

(1)

A
q
-adic
uniformization datum

(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0},i_{D},i_{D\cdot q})

for
(O_{D},\ast)
is an
(O_{D},\ast)
-triple
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0})
as above, along with a choice of isomorphisms of algebras with involution:

i_{D}:\left(\End(H,B_{D}),\dagger\right)\xrightarrow{\sim}(M_{2}(B_{D}),\ast_{D}),

i_{D\cdot q}:\left(\End^{0}(\overline{A}_{0},\overline{\iota}_{0}),\dagger\right)\xrightarrow{\sim}(M_{2}(B_{D\cdot q}),\ast_{D\cdot q}).

(2)

A
q
-adic
uniformization datum

(\ast,A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0},i_{D},i_{D\cdot q})
for
V_{D}
is a choice of positive nebentype involution
\ast
on
O_{D}
– of unit type if
q|D
– and a uniformization datum
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0},i_{D},i_{D\cdot q})
for
(O_{D},\ast)
.

Recall here that
V_{D}
was defined in (
1.2
).

Remark 1.4.5
.

(1)

Given any
(O_{D},\ast)
-triple
(A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0})
, the choices
in Definition
1.4.4
(
1
) exist by Lemma
1.2.6
.

(2)

Given a
q
-adic uniformization datum
(\ast,A_{0},\iota_{0},\lambda_{0},i_{D},i_{D\cdot q})
for
V_{D}
, we also obtain isomorphisms

\End(H,B_{D})^{\dagger=1,\tr=0}\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{D},\;\End^{0}(\overline{A}_{0},\overline{\iota}_{0})^{\dagger=1,\tr=0}\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{D\cdot q}.

The former determines an isomorphism

G_{\mathcal{D}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\spin(V_{D}).

Moreover, the action of
\End(\overline{A}_{0},\overline{\iota}_{0})
on

H_{1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}(\overline{A}_{0},\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q})\cong H_{1}(A_{0}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q})

induces an isomorphism
V_{D\cdot q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q}\cong V_{D}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q}
. In turn, this induces an isomorphism
\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q})\cong\spin(V_{D\cdot q})(\mathbb{A}_{f}^{q})
.

1.5.
Local conditions and Selmer groups

1.5.1.

In this subsection,
O
is the ring of integers of a finite extension
E/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and
\varpi\in O
is a uniformizer.

Notation 1.5.2
.

(1)

Suppose
M
is a finite free
O
-module with an action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
, where
\ell
may be equal to
p
. We consider the Bloch-Kato local conditions

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\coloneqq\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\to\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})}{H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})}\right).

(2)

Globally, if
M
is a finite free
O
-module with
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-action, let

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},M)\coloneqq\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},M)\to\prod_{\ell}\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)}{H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)}\right).

(3)

Suppose
\ell\neq p
. For an unramified, finitely generated
O
-module
M
(either finite or infinite) with
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-action, we let

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\coloneqq H^{1}_{{\operatorname{unr}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M).

Remark 1.5.3
.

Notations
1.5.2
(
1
,
3
) are consistent because, when
M
is free over
O
and unramified as a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-module, the map

H^{1}(I_{\ell},M)\to H^{1}(I_{\ell},M\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is injective.

1.5.4.

Fix integers
a\leq 0\leq b
.
Recall that a finite
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module
M
is said to be
torsion crystalline
with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
if there exists a crystalline
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module
V
with Hodge Tate weights in
[a,b]
, and two
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-stable lattices
T_{1}\subset T_{2}\subset V
, such that
M=T_{2}/T_{1}.
A finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module
M
is torsion crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
if
M/p^{n}
is so for all
n\geq 1
.

If
M
is torsion crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
, let

H^{1}_{f,{\operatorname{tors}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)

be the subspace of cohomology classes such that the corresponding extension

0\to M\to\ast\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to 0

is torsion crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
.
If
M
is an
O[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module, then
H^{1}_{f,{\operatorname{tors}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
is an
O
-submodule of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
.

Proposition 1.5.5
.

A finite free
O[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module
M
is torsion crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
if and only if
M\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
. In particular, if
M
is a finite free
O[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-module, then
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)=H^{1}_{f,{\operatorname{tors}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
.

Proof.

This is
[
65
, §7.3]
.
5
5

5

In fact, for the main results it suffices to assume
b-a<p-1
, in which case Proposition
1.5.5
is due to Breuil
[
14
, Proposition 6]
.

∎

Notation 1.5.6
.

We will from now on write
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
in place of
H^{1}_{f,{\operatorname{tors}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
for any finitely generated
O
-module
M
with
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-action; by Proposition
1.5.5
, this is consistent with Notation
1.5.2
(
1
).

1.6.
Duality

1.6.1.

Let
\ell
be a prime, possibly equal to
p
.
If
M
is a finite free
O
-module with
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-action, let
M^{\ast}=\Hom_{O}(M,O(1))
. Similarly, if
M
is a locally compact
O
-module with
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-action, let
M^{\vee}=\Hom_{O}(M,E/O(1))
be the Cartier dual.
We have the local Tate pairings

(1.4)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\ell}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\vee})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},E/O(1))=E/O

and

(1.5)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\ell}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},O(1))=O.

The former is perfect, and the latter is perfect modulo torsion.
We recall the following standard fact.

Lemma 1.6.2
.

Suppose
M
is a finite free
O
-module with
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-action.
Then the subspaces

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
and
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})
pair to zero under the local Tate pairing (
1.5
).

Proof.

When
O=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
the result follows from
[
9
, Proposition 3.8]
.
6
6

6

Although
loc. cit.
assumes
M\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is de Rham when
\ell=p
, this is needed to show that
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
and
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})
are
exact
annihilators; the proof shows that they annihilate each other in general.

To reduce to this case, note that
M^{\ast}
is canonically isomorphic to
M^{\prime}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(M,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, and we have a commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\times H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\times H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{p}}=0}

Since
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M^{\ast})
is
O
-stable and the trace pairing
O\times O\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is nondegenerate, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 1.6.3
.

Fix integers
a\leq 0\leq b
, and let
M
be a finite free
O
-module with
[G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}]
-action which is torsion crystalline with Hodge-Tate weights in
[a,b]
. Then:

(1)

We have
\varprojlim H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})=H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)
.

(2)

For all
n\geq 1
, there exists
m\geq n
such that

\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{m})\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\right)=\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\right).

(3)

For all
n\geq 1
, there exists
m\geq n
such that the local Tate pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{\ast}/\varpi^{n})\to O/\varpi^{n}

vanishes when restricted to

\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{m})\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\right)\times\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{\ast}/\varpi^{m})\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{\ast}/\varpi^{n})\right).

Remark 1.6.4
.

When
2(b-a)\leq p-2
and
O=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we may take
m=n
in the final part by
[
85
, Corollary 6.1]
.

Proof.

The first part is clear from Proposition
1.5.5
, and it follows that

\cap_{m\geq n}\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{m})\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\right)=\im\left(H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n}\right).

Since
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})
is a finite
O
-module,
it is clear that (
2
) holds.

For (
3
), take
m
sufficiently large to satisfy (
2
) for both
M
and
M^{\ast}
. The assertion then follows from Lemma
1.6.2
and the commutativity of the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{\ast})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M/\varpi^{n})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M^{\ast}/\varpi^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O/\varpi^{n}.}

∎

2.
Automorphic representations, Hecke algebras, and Shimura varieties

2.1.
Hecke algebras and Galois representations

Let
G
be a split, connected, reductive algebraic group over
\mathbb{Z}[S_{0}^{-1}]
for a finite set of primes
S_{0}
, with Borel subgroup
B=TU\subset G
and Weyl group
W_{G}
. For simplicity, we assume that the derived subgroup of
G
is simply connected.

Definition 2.1.1
.

(1)

For a prime
\ell\not\in S_{0}
and a ring
R
, let
\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}
denote the spherical Hecke algebra of compactly supported,
R
-valued,
G(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
-biinvariant functions on
G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
.
For a finite set
S\supset S_{0}
and a ring
R
,

\mathbb{T}_{G,R}^{S}\coloneqq\bigotimes^{\prime}_{\ell\not\in S}\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}.

(2)

Let
P=MN\subset G
be a parabolic subgroup. For all
\ell\not\in S_{0}
, we define a natural map

S_{M}^{G}:\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}\to\mathbb{T}_{M,\ell,R}

by

S_{M}^{G}(f)(m)=\int_{N(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})}f(mn)\mathrm{d}n,

where the Haar measure on
N(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
gives volume 1 to
N(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
.

When
R=\mathbb{Z}
, we drop it from the notation.

Proposition 2.1.2
.

We have a commutative diagram of functors

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[M(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})]-\operatorname{Mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{M,\ell,R}-\operatorname{Mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[G(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})]-\operatorname{Mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}-\operatorname{Mod},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{M(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\natural-\Ind}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{M}^{G})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{G(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})}}

where
\natural-\Ind
denotes the unnormalized parabolic induction.

Proof.

This follows from combining Lemmas 2.4, 2.7, and 2.9 of
[
83
]
.
∎

Notation 2.1.3
.

Let
\widehat{T}\subset\widehat{G}
be the dual torus to
T
. We write
X^{\bullet}
and
X_{\bullet}
for the character and cocharacter groups of any split algebraic torus.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Suppose now that
R
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\ell^{1/2},\ell^{-1/2}]
-algebra
and recall the Satake transform

(2.2)

\begin{split}R[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]^{W_{G}}&\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}\\
\lambda&\mapsto[c_{\lambda}].\end{split}

(1)

If
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}
is a maximal ideal with residue field
k
, the
Satake parameter
for
\mathfrak{m}
is the unique element

\Sat_{G,\ell}(\mathfrak{m})\in\widehat{T}(\overline{k})/W_{G}

such that, for any
\lambda\in R[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]^{W_{G}}
,

\lambda(\Sat_{G,\ell}(\mathfrak{m}))=[c_{\lambda}]\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}.
A maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{G,R}
defines a maximal ideal of each
\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}
with
\ell\not\in S
; we denote by
\Sat_{G,\ell}(\mathfrak{m})
the corresponding element of
\widehat{T}(\overline{k})/W_{G}
for each
\ell\not\in S
.

(2)

Let
\rho_{G}\in X_{\bullet}(\widehat{T})=X^{\bullet}(T)
be the half-sum of positive roots. A
normalization
of the Satake transform for
G
(cf.
[
42
, §8]
) is a choice of element
\omega_{G}\in X_{\bullet}(Z_{\widehat{G}})
such that

\omega_{G}\equiv\rho_{G}\pmod{2X_{\bullet}(\widehat{T})}.

2.1.5.

For the rest of this subsection, we assume that
R
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
-algebra, with a fixed choice of square root
\ell^{1/2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p^{2}}
for any
\ell\neq p
.

Definition 2.1.6
.

Let
S\supset S_{0}
be a finite set of primes.

(1)

Given a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{G,R}
with residue field
k
, a semisimple Galois representation

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\widehat{G}(\overline{k})

is said to be
associated
to
\mathfrak{m}
(with respect to a normalization
\omega_{G}
of the Satake transform) if for all but finitely many
\ell\not\in S
,
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}}
is unramified and

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Frob_{\ell^{-1}})^{ss}\sim\omega_{G}(\ell^{-1/2})\cdot\Sat_{G,\ell}(\mathfrak{m});

here
\sim
denotes
\widehat{G}(\overline{k})
-conjugacy.
If this holds for
all

\ell\not\in S\cup\left\{p\right\}
, then
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is said to be
strongly associated
with
\mathfrak{m}
.

(2)

If there exists a Galois representation (strongly) associated to
\mathfrak{m}
, then
\mathfrak{m}
is said to be (strongly) of
Galois type
.

Note that whether
\mathfrak{m}
is (strongly) of Galois type is independent of the choice of normalization; the corresponding representations
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
differ by the composite of
\chi_{p}^{\operatorname{cyc}}
and an algebraic cocharacter of
Z_{\widehat{G}}
.

Example 2.1.7
.

Suppose
\pi
is an automorphic representation of
\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{A})
, unramified outside
S
, and fix an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
. This determines a distinguished square root
\ell^{1/2}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
for all
\ell
. Moreover, the Hecke action on
\iota^{-1}\pi^{S}
defines a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}\subset\mathbb{T}^{S}_{\GSP_{4},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
If
\pi
is relevant in the sense of Definition
2.2.5
below, then the representation
\rho_{\pi,\iota}
of Theorem
2.2.10
is strongly associated to
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}
with respect to the normalization given by the scalar subgroup
\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow\GSP_{4}
.

Definition 2.1.8
.

Let
S\supset S_{0}
be a finite set of primes. A maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{G,R}^{S}
with residue field
k
is said to be
Eisenstein
if there exists an associated
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
(with respect to any normalization) which factors as

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\widehat{M}(\overline{k})\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}(\overline{k})

for some standard parabolic subgroup
P=MN\subsetneq G
.

Proposition 2.1.9
.

Let
S\supset S_{0}
be a finite set of primes.
Suppose for some proper parabolic subgroup
P=MN\subsetneq G
,
\mathfrak{m}_{M}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{M,R}
is a maximal ideal of Galois type.
Then

\mathfrak{m}=(S_{M}^{G})^{\ast}\mathfrak{m}_{M}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{G,R}
is Eisenstein.

Proof.

Fix normalizations
\omega_{M}
and
\omega_{G}
of the Satake transform for
M
and
G
, respectively. Also let
d_{P}\in X_{\bullet}(Z_{\widehat{M}})
be the
dual of the character

\det(\ad(-)|N):M\to\mathbb{G}_{m}.

By the well-known compatibility of the Satake transform with
normalized
parabolic induction, combined with Proposition
2.1.2
,
\Sat_{G,\ell}(\mathfrak{m})\in\widehat{T}(\overline{k})/W_{G}
is represented by any representative of
\Sat_{M,\ell}(\mathfrak{m}_{M})\cdot d_{P}(\ell^{1/2})\in\widehat{T}(\overline{k})/W_{M}.
It follows that

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneqq\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}_{M}}\otimes(\chi_{p}^{\operatorname{cyc}})^{\alpha}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\widehat{M}(\overline{k})\hookrightarrow\widehat{G}(\overline{k})

is associated to
\mathfrak{m}
, where

\alpha\coloneqq\frac{\omega_{G}-\omega_{M}-d_{P}}{2}\in X_{\bullet}(Z_{\widehat{M}}).

Hence
\mathfrak{m}
is Eisenstein.

∎

2.1.10.
The case of
G=\GSP_{4}

When
G=\GSP_{4}
, the Hecke algebra
\mathbb{T}_{G,\ell,R}
can be identified with the polynomial algebra
R[T_{\ell,1},T_{\ell,2},\langle\ell\rangle]
, where the generators correspond to the following double coset operators:

\displaystyle T_{\ell,1}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left(\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\begin{pmatrix}\ell&&&\\
&1&&\\
&&\ell^{-1}&\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\right),

\displaystyle T_{\ell,2}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left(\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\begin{pmatrix}\ell&&&\\
&\ell&&\\
&&1&\\
&&&1\end{pmatrix}\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\right),

\displaystyle\langle\ell\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left(\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\begin{pmatrix}\ell&&&\\
&\ell&&\\
&&\ell&\\
&&&\ell\end{pmatrix}\GSP_{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})\right).

The Satake parameter of a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbb{T}_{\GSP_{4},\ell,R}
with residue field
k

can be identified with the data of an element
\nu\in k
– the similitude factor of
\Sat_{\GSP_{4},\ell}(\mathfrak{m})
– together with the multi-set

\left\{\alpha,\beta,\nu/\alpha,\nu/\beta\right\}
of elements of
\overline{k}
– the eigenvalues of
\Sat_{\GSP_{4},\ell}(\mathfrak{m})
in the standard four-dimensional representation of
\widehat{\GSP_{4}}=\GSP_{4}
.
We will abusively write the Satake parameter as
\left\{\alpha,\beta,\nu/\alpha,\nu/\beta\right\}
; in general,
\nu
is not always determined by this unordered set.

The relation to Hecke eigenvalues is given explicitly in this case by

(2.3)

\begin{split}T_{\ell,1}&=\ell^{2}\left(\alpha\beta/\nu+\alpha/\beta+\beta/\alpha+\nu/\alpha\beta\right)+(\ell^{2}-1)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}\\
T_{\ell,2}&=\ell^{3/2}\left(\alpha+\beta+\nu/\alpha+\nu/\beta\right)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}\\
\langle\ell\rangle&=\nu\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}.\end{split}

When it is clear from context that
G=\GSP_{4}
, the subscript
G
in Hecke algebras and Satake parameters may be omitted from the notation.

2.2.
Automorphic forms and Galois representations

Let
F
be a totally real field. If we fix a prime
p
and an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
, then to each archimedean place
v|\infty
of
F
, we can associate an embedding
\iota^{\ast}v:F\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Theorem 2.2.1
.

Suppose
\pi
is a unitary, cuspidal automorphic representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
associated to a Hilbert modular form of weight
(2k_{v})_{v|\infty}
, where
v
runs over archimedean places of
F
and
k_{v}\geq 1
for all
v
. Then for every isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
, with
p
a prime, there exists a Galois representation

\rho_{\pi,\iota}:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

with the following properties.

(1)

\rho_{\pi,\iota}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is potentially semistable for all
v|p
, and for all nonarchimedean primes
v
of
F
:

\iota WD(\rho_{\pi,\iota}|_{G_{F_{v}}})^{F-ss}\simeq\rec(\pi_{v}\otimes|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Moreover this Weil-Deligne representation is pure of weight
-1
.

(2)

For each place
v|\infty
of
F
,
the Hodge-Tate weights of
\rho_{\pi,\iota}
with respect to the embedding
\iota^{\ast}v:F\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
are
1-{k_{v}}
and
{k_{v}}
.

(3)

The similitude character of
\rho_{\pi,\iota}
is
\iota^{-1}\rec(\omega_{\pi})\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}
, where
\omega_{\pi}
is the central character.

Here
\rec
is the usual local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
, normalized to coincide with the reciprocity map of local class field theory when
n=1
.

Proof.

Note that the purity in (
1
) follows immediately from the claimed identity of Weil-Deligne representations and the Ramanujan conjecture for
\pi

[
8
]
.

The existence of
\rho_{\pi,\iota}
with the property (
1
) for all
v\nmid p
was proved by Carayol
[
19
]
under the assumption that either
[F:\mathbb{Q}]
is odd, or
\pi_{v}
is square-integrable for some finite prime
v
of
F
. In general,
\rho_{\pi,\iota}
was constructed by Taylor
[
108
]
, and the proof of (
1
) for
v\nmid p
follows the argument of
[
128
, Theorem 2.1.3]
