(3)

•

If
\pi
is not endoscopic, then
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is absolutely irreducible.

•

If
\pi
is endoscopic associated to a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
of automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
, then
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1},\mathfrak{p}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{2},\mathfrak{p}}
are both absolutely irreducible.

Remark 4.1.2
.

Assumption
4.1.1
(
2
) implies that
p>5
.

Notation 4.1.3
.

Under Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
):

(1)

Let
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
be an
O_{\mathfrak{p}}[G_{\mathbb{Q}}]
-module such that
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}=V_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
; Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
) implies that
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is unique up to isomorphism.

(2)

For all
n\geq 1
, we write
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p},n}\coloneqq T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}/\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{n}T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
. Also let
\overline{T}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\coloneqq T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p},1}
.

(3)

When
\mathfrak{p}
is clear from context, it may be dropped from the above notations.

Remark 4.1.4
.

Under Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
),
T_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is isomorphic to its
O_{\mathfrak{p}}
-dual; we use this to view
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
as valued in
\GSP_{4}(O_{\mathfrak{p}})
, and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
as valued in
\GSP_{4}(k_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

Lemma 4.1.5
.

Assumption
4.1.1
holds for all but finitely many primes
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
.

Proof.

That Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
) holds for all but finitely many primes
\mathfrak{p}
follows from
[
124
, Theorem 1.2(i)]
in the non-endoscopic case; in the endoscopic case, it follows from
[
95
, Theorem 2.1]
. It is also obvious that Assumption
4.1.1
(
1
) holds for cofinitely many
\mathfrak{p}
.
We consider Assumption
4.1.1
(
2
).

First fix an arbitrary
\mathfrak{p}
with residue characteristic
p
. The image of
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
contains an element with distinct eigenvalues by
[
100
, Theorem 1]
and Theorem
2.2.10
(
2
). Hence by the Chebotarev density theorem, there exists a prime
\ell\not\in S\cup\left\{p\right\}
such that
\Frob_{\ell}
has distinct eigenvalues on
\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
. The Satake parameter of
\pi_{\ell}
is therefore multiplicity-free and of the form
\left\{\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{-1},\beta^{-1}\right\}
with
|\alpha|=|\beta|=1
.
Let
E_{1}=E_{0}(\alpha,\beta)
, which is a finite extension because the Hecke eigenvalues of
\pi_{\ell}
lie in
E_{0}
. For all but finitely many primes
\mathfrak{p}_{1}\nmid\ell
of
E_{1}
, we will have

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle\not\equiv 1,\ell,\ell^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}

\displaystyle\beta^{2}

\displaystyle\not\equiv 1,\ell,\ell^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}

\displaystyle\alpha\beta

\displaystyle\not\equiv 1,\ell,\ell^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}

\displaystyle\alpha/\beta

\displaystyle\not\equiv 1,\ell,\ell^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{1}},

\displaystyle\ell^{4}

\displaystyle\not\equiv 1,\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{1}}.

For such a
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
, let
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{p}_{1}|_{E_{0}}
and let
p^{\prime}
be the residue characteristic of
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
. Then we have
\ell^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p^{\prime}}
and the eigenvalues of
\Frob_{\ell}
on
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}
are
distinct and not of ratio
\ell
, i.e. Assumption
4.1.1
(
2
) holds for
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.6
.

Assume
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
). Then:

(1)

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
is
\varpi
-torsion-free.

(2)

Suppose given
c\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
and
a\geq 1

such that
c\not\in\varpi^{a}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
. Then for all
n\geq 1
, the image
c_{n}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})
satisfies

{\operatorname{ord}}_{\varpi}c_{n}>n-a.

Proof.

The assumption implies that
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q},\overline{T}_{\pi})=0
. The long exact sequence in Galois cohomology associated to

0\to T_{\pi}\xrightarrow{\varpi}T_{\pi}\to\overline{T}_{\pi}\to 0

therefore gives (
1
).
For (
2
), a similar argument to (
1
) shows that the map
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,a})\xrightarrow{\varpi^{n-a}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})
is injective, so the
kernel of

\varpi^{n-a}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})

coincides with the kernel of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,a})
. Hence it suffices to show that
c_{a}\neq 0
, which is clear from the assumption
c\not\in\varpi^{a}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
and the long exact sequence in Galois cohomology associated to

0\to T_{\pi}\xrightarrow{\varpi^{a}}T_{\pi}\to T_{\pi,a}\to 0.

∎

Lemma 4.1.7
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
. Then
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\subset\mathbb{T}^{S}_{O}
is non-Eisenstein and generic.

Proof.

Recall from Remark
4.1.4
that
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
is valued in
\GSP_{4}(k)
. Then
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
is clearly of Galois type associated to
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
.
The genericity of
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
(Definition
2.7.3
) therefore follows from Assumption
4.1.1
(
2
).
From Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
), it follows that
\overline{T}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\otimes\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
contains no Galois-stable line. So if
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
were Eisenstein,
\overline{\rho}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
would have to factor through a Siegel parabolic subgroup. In particular, then
\overline{T}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\otimes\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}=\rho_{0}\oplus\rho_{0}\otimes\det\rho_{0}^{-1}\otimes\omega_{p}
, where
\rho_{0}:G_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\GL_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is some irreducible representation, and
\omega_{p}
is the mod-
p
cyclotomic character. This is clearly impossible by Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
) and Lemma
4.1.8
below.
∎

Lemma 4.1.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}|p
be a prime of
E_{0}
such that
p>5
and
\pi_{p}
is unramified. If
\pi
is endoscopic associated to
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, then
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{1}}
and
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{2}}
are not isomorphic.

Proof.

Recall that
\rho_{\pi_{1}}
and
\rho_{\pi_{2}}
have Hodge-Tate weights
\left\{-1,2\right\}
and
\left\{0,1\right\}
up to reording, by Lemma
2.2.9
and Theorem
2.2.1
(
2
). The lemma then follows from Fontaine-Laffaille theory
[
32
, Théorème 6.1]
.
∎

4.2.
Admissible primes

In this section,
\mathfrak{p}|p
is a prime of
E_{0}
.

Definition 4.2.1
.

(1)

We say a prime
q\not\in S\cup\left\{p\right\}
is
admissible
for
\rho_{\pi}=\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
if
q^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and the generalized eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}(\Frob_{q})
are of the form
\left\{q,\alpha,1,q/\alpha\right\}
, with
\alpha\neq\pm q,\pm 1,q^{2},q^{-1}
.

(2)

If
q
is admissible, define
n(q)\geq 1
to be the greatest integer such that
\det(\Frob_{q}-q|V_{\pi})\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi^{n(q)}}
.

(3)

We say
q
is
n
-
admissible
if it is admissible and
n(q)\geq n
.

(4)

If
Q\geq 1
is squarefree, we say
Q
is
admissible
(resp.
n
-
admissible
) if all primes
q|Q
are so.

(5)

Analogously, an element
g\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}
is called
admissible
for
\rho_{\pi}
if
\nu_{g}\coloneqq\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}(g)
satisfies
\nu_{g}^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
, and

g
acts on
V_{\pi}
with generalized eigenvalues
\left\{\nu_{g},1,\alpha,\nu_{g}/\alpha\right\}
for some

\alpha\not\equiv\pm\nu_{g},\pm 1,\nu_{g}^{2},\nu_{g}^{-1}\pmod{\varpi}.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies
Assumption
4.1.1
(
3
). Then
a prime
q
is
n
-admissible for
\rho_{\pi}=\rho_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}
if and only if there exists a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}}
-stable decomposition

T_{\pi,n}=M_{0,n}\oplus M_{1,n}

such that:

(1)

M_{0,n}
and
M_{1,n}
are each free of rank two over
O/\varpi^{n}
, and

\Frob_{q}|_{M_{0,n}}=\begin{pmatrix}q&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}
in some basis.

(2)

\Frob_{q}^{2}-1
,
\Frob_{q}^{2}-q^{2}
,
\Frob_{q}-q^{2}
, and
\Frob_{q}-q^{-1}
all act invertibly on
M_{1,n}
.

Proof.

Immediate from Definition
4.2.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime of
E_{0}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There exist admissible primes for
\rho_{\pi}
.

(2)

For all
n
, there exist
n
-admissible primes for
\rho_{\pi}.

(3)

There exists an admissible element
g\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}
for
\rho_{\pi}
.

Proof.

Clearly (
2
) implies (
1
), and (
3
) implies (
2
) by the Chebotarev density theorem. The proof that (
1
) implies (
2
) follows the same argument of
[
70
, Lemma 2.7.1]
, and (
2
) implies (
3
) by compactness.
∎

4.2.4.

Now suppose
\pi
is endoscopic associated to a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
of automorphic representations of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
.

Definition 4.2.5
.

(1)

A prime
q\not\in S{\cup}\left\{p\right\}

is called
BD-admissible
for
\rho_{\pi_{i}}=\rho_{\pi_{i},\mathfrak{p}}
, with
i=1
or
2
,
if
q^{2}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and the generalized eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{i}}(\Frob_{q})
are
\left\{1,q\right\}
.

(2)

If
q
is BD-admissible for
\rho_{\pi_{i}}
, define
n(q)\geq 1
to be the greatest integer such that
\debt(\Frob_{q}-q|V_{\pi_{i}})=\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi^{n(q)}}.

(3)

We say
q
is
n
-BD-admissible
if it is BD-admissible and
n(q)\geq 1
.

(4)

If
Q\geq 1
is squarefree, we say
Q
is BD-admissible (resp
n
-BD-admissible) for
\rho_{\pi_{i}}
if all
q|Q
are so.

(5)

Likewise, an element
g\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}
is called BD-admissible for
\rho_{\pi_{i}}
if
\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}(g)^{2}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and
g
acts on
V_{\pi_{i}}
with eigenvalues
\chi_{p,{\operatorname{cyc}}}(g)
and 1.

Remark 4.2.6
.

(1)

Definition
4.2.5
is adapted from
[
7
, p. 18]
, but there it is allowed that the eigenvalues of
\Frob_{q}
on
\overline{\rho}_{\pi_{i}}
are
-1
and
-q
.

(2)

If
q

is
n
-admissible for
\rho_{\pi}
, then it is
n
-BD-admissible for exactly one of
\rho_{\pi_{1}}
and
\rho_{\pi_{2}}
; and if
g\in G_{\mathbb{Q}}
is admissible for
\rho_{\pi}
, then it is BD-admissible for exactly one of
\rho_{\pi_{1}}
and
\rho_{\pi_{2}}
.
In particular, if
Q\geq 1
is admissible for
\rho_{\pi}
, there is a unique factorization
Q=Q_{1}\cdot Q_{2}
with
Q_{1},Q_{2}\geq 1
, such that all
q|Q_{i}
are BD-admissible for
\rho_{\pi_{i}}
.

4.2.7.

For any prime
q\not\in S\cup\left\{p\right\}
,
recall that
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})=H^{1}_{{\operatorname{unr}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
, and set
H^{1}_{/f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})/H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
.

Proposition 4.2.8
.

Suppose
q
is
n
-admissible. Then
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
and
H^{1}_{/f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
are each free of rank one over
\mathcal{O}/\varpi^{n}
, and local Poitou-Tate duality induces a perfect pairing

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})\times H^{1}_{/f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})\to O/\varpi^{n}.

Proof.

First note that the induced pairing is perfect because
H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
is self-annihilating under the Tate pairing, and one can check
\lg_{O}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})=2\lg_{O}H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})
using the local Euler characteristic formula and local duality. So it suffices to prove that

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})=T_{\pi,n}/(\Frob_{q}-1)T_{\pi,n}

is free of rank one over
O/\varpi^{n}
.
Indeed, this is immediate from Lemma
4.2.2
.
∎

Notation 4.2.9
.

If
q
is
n
-admissible and
S^{\prime}\supset S
is a finite set with
q\not\in S^{\prime}
, then by Proposition
4.2.8
we have the localization and residue maps:

\displaystyle\loc_{q}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}^{S^{\prime}}/\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})

\displaystyle\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})\simeq O/\varpi^{n}

\displaystyle\partial_{q}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})

\displaystyle\to H^{1}_{/f}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},T_{\pi,n})\simeq O/\varpi^{n}

4.3.
Level structures and test vectors

Fix a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
of residue characteristic
p
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

For any squarefree
D\geq 1
, an
S
-level structure
for
\spin(V_{D})
is a compact open subgroup
K=\prod K_{\ell}\subset\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such that:

(1)

K
is neat in the sense of
[
90
, §0.1]
.

(2)

For all
\ell\not\in S\cup\div(D)
,
K_{\ell}
is hyperspecial.

An
S
-tidy level structure
is an
S
-level structure satisfying:

(3)

If
K_{Z}\subset\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}
is the intersection of
K
with the center of
\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, then the finite group
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}/K_{Z}
has order coprime to
p
.

The reason for the final condition of Definition
4.3.1
is the following convenient lemma:

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Suppose
K
is an
S
-tidy level structure for
\spin(V_{D})
.
Then for all finite sets
S^{\prime}\supset S
and all
\ell\not\in S\cup\div(D)
, we have

\langle\ell\rangle=1\text{ on }H^{\ast}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})(\mathbb{C}),O)_{\mathfrak{m}^{S^{\prime}}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}}.

Proof.

By Definition
4.3.1
(
3
), after replacing
O
by a finite extension we can write

H^{\ast}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})(\mathbb{C}),O)=\bigoplus_{\chi}H^{\ast}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})(\mathbb{C}),O)_{\chi},

where
\chi
runs over
O
-valued characters of the finite group
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}/K_{Z}
and

\langle\ell\rangle=\chi(\ell)\text{ on }H^{\ast}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})(\mathbb{C}),O)_{\chi}.

The characters
\chi
are distinct modulo
\varpi
, and the lemma follows because
\pi
has trivial central character.
∎

Definition 4.3.3
.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree, let
K
be
an
S
-level structure for
\spin(V_{D})
, and let
R=O
or
O/\varpi^{n}
, viewed as a
\mathbb{T}^{S}_{O}
-module via the Hecke eigenvalues of
\pi
. We define
\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,R)
as follows.

(1)

If
\sigma(D)
is
even
and
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
, then

\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,R)=\Hom_{{\mathbb{T}}^{S\cup\div(D)}_{O}[G_{\mathbb{Q}}]}\left(H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2)),T_{\pi}\otimes_{O}R\right).

(2)

If
\sigma(D)
is
odd
, then

\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,R)=\Hom_{{\mathbb{T}}^{S\cup\div(D)}_{O}}\left(O\left[\Sh_{K}(V_{D})\right],R\right).

Remark 4.3.4
.

The definition of
\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,R)
depends only on
K
, and not on
S
; one can check this using Theorem
2.4.6
(
3
), and Corollary
2.7.8
when
\sigma(D)
is even.

4.4.
Constructions

Fix a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
of residue characteristic
p
.

Construction 4.4.1
.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree, and let
Q\geq 1
be admissible and coprime to
D
, such that
\sigma(DQ)
is
odd
.
For any
S
-level structure
K
for
\spin(V_{DQ})
:

(1)

We define

\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q;K)\subset O/\varpi^{n}

to be the submodule spanned by the elements
\alpha(z)
, where:

\circ

\alpha
lies in
\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,O/\varpi^{n})
.

\circ

z
lies in
{\operatorname{SC}}^{2}_{K}(V_{DQ},O)
(Notation
3.1.5
).

(2)

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K}
is a test function, then we define

\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)\subset\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q;K)

to be the submodule spanned by elements
\alpha(Z(T,\varphi)_{K})
, where:

\circ

\alpha
lies in
\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,O/\varpi^{n})
.

\circ

T
lies in
\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
, and
Z(T,\varphi)_{K}
was defined in Construction
3.1.2
.

(3)

If
Q=1
, then we define
\lambda^{D}(1;K)\subset O
and
\lambda^{D}(1,\varphi;K)\subset\lambda^{D}(1;K)
analogously, where now
\alpha
ranges over
\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,O)
.

(4)

We write
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q)\subset O/\varpi^{n}
for the submodule spanned by
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q;K)
as
K
varies, and likewise
\lambda^{D}(1)
.

In all of the above constructions, we include a subscript
\mathfrak{p}
only when it is necessary for clarity.

4.4.2.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree with
\sigma(D)

even
, and suppose
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
. Let
\mathfrak{m}\coloneqq\mathfrak{m}^{S\cup\div(D)}_{\pi,\mathfrak{p}}\subset\mathbb{T}^{S\cup\div(D)}_{O}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.1.7
and Theorem
2.7.5
(
2
) that the étale realization map

\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D}),O)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to H^{4}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}^{G_{\mathbb{Q}}}

is trivial. We therefore obtain a well-defined Abel-Jacobi map

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}:\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D}),O)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}).

For any
\alpha\in\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,R)
with
R=O
or
O/\varpi^{n}
, we obtain an induced map

(4.1)

\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D}),O)_{\mathfrak{m}}\xrightarrow{\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\ast}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi}\otimes_{O}R).

Construction 4.4.3
.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree, and let
Q\geq 1
be admissible and coprime to
D
, such that
\sigma(DQ)
is
even
. Suppose
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
, and let
K
be an
S
-level structure for
\spin(V_{DQ})
.

(1)

We define

\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q;K)\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})

to be the submodule spanned by
\alpha_{\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(z)
, where:

\circ

\alpha
lies in
\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,O/\varpi^{n})
.

\circ

z
lies in
{\operatorname{SC}}^{2}_{K}(V_{DQ},O)
(Notation
3.1.5
).

(2)

For any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K}
, we define

\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)\subset\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q;K)

to be the submodule spanned by elements
\alpha_{\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{K})
, where:

\circ

\alpha
lies in
\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,O/\varpi^{n})
.

\circ

T
lies in
\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
, and
Z(T,\varphi)_{K}
was defined in Construction
3.1.2
.

(3)

If
Q=1
, then we define
\kappa^{D}(1;K)\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
and
\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)\subset\kappa^{D}(1;K)
analogously, with now
\alpha\in\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,O).

(4)

We write
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q)\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi,n})
for the submodule spanned by
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q;K)
as
K
varies, and likewise
\kappa^{D}(1)
.

In all of the above constructions, we include a subscript
\mathfrak{p}
only when it is necessary for clarity.

Remark 4.4.4
.

The only reason to distinguish between
D
and
Q
in Constructions
4.4.1
and
4.4.3
is to define
\lambda^{D}(1)
and
\kappa^{D}(1)
when
\sigma(D)
is odd and even, respectively; moreover, one can check using Corollary
2.5.3
that
\lambda^{D}(1)
or
\kappa^{D}(1)
is trivial unless
\pi_{\ell}
is transferrable for all
\ell|D
.

Now we prove some basic properties of Constructions
4.4.1
and
4.4.3
.

Proposition 4.4.5
.

Suppose
L(1/2,\pi,\operatorname{spin})\neq 0
.
If
D>1
is squarefree with
\sigma(D)
odd and

\pi_{f}^{D}
can be completed to an automorphic representation of
\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A})
, then for any prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
,
\lambda^{D}(1)_{\mathfrak{p}}\neq 0
. Moreover, for all but finitely many
\mathfrak{p}
, we have

\lambda^{D}(1)_{\mathfrak{p}}\not\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}.

Proof.

Let
\Pi
be any automorphic representation of
\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A})
with
\Pi_{f}^{D}\simeq\pi^{D}_{f}
.
Because
\pi
has trivial central character,
\Pi
descends to an automorphic representation of
\SO(V_{D})(\mathbb{A})
.
By
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
, the global theta lift of
\Pi
to
\MP_{4}(\mathbb{A})
is nonzero; i.e., the map

(4.2)

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A},\mathbb{C})\otimes\Pi&\to\operatorname{Fun}(\SP_{4}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\MP_{4}(\mathbb{A}),\mathbb{C})\\
\varphi\otimes\alpha&\mapsto\theta_{\varphi}(\alpha)\end{split}

is not identically zero (Notation
3.4.1
). Also, the image of (
4.2
) lies in the space of cusp forms by the global tower property of the theta lift
[
92
]
: otherwise,
\Pi
would occur in the restriction to
\SO(V_{D})(\mathbb{A})
of the theta lift of an automorphic representation of
\MP_{2}(\mathbb{A})
, which is ruled out by the Shimura-Waldspurger correspondence and the relevance of
\pi
. In particular, by the local-global compatibility of the theta correspondence (see the proof of
[
92
, Theorem I.2.2]
), if
\varphi=\otimes\varphi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A},\mathbb{C})
and
\alpha=\otimes\alpha_{v}\in\Pi
are factorizable, then

(4.3)

\theta_{\varphi}(\alpha)\neq 0\iff\langle\rho_{v}(\varphi_{v}),\alpha_{v}\rangle\neq 0\;\forall v,

where by definition

\rho_{v}:\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{C})\to(\Pi_{v})^{\vee}\boxtimes\Theta_{v}(\Pi_{v})

is the maximal
(\Pi_{v})^{\vee}
-isotypic quotient of the Weil representation of
\SO(V_{D})(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\times\MP_{4}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
on
\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{v},\mathbb{C})
.

When
v=\infty
, then
\alpha_{\infty}\in\Pi_{\infty}
is unique up to scalar, and we take
\varphi_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C})
to be the Gaussian from Lemma
3.4.3
. Then
\alpha_{\infty}
and
\varphi_{\infty}
satisfy the local condition in (
4.3
) by the theory of joint harmonics
[
1
, Proposition 2.1(2), §5]
.
In particular, (
4.3
) implies that we can fix an
S
-level structure
K
and data

\alpha\in\Hom_{\operatorname{set}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D}),O_{E_{0}})\cap\Pi\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\spin(V_{D})(\mathbb{A}),\mathbb{C}),\;\;\varphi_{f}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O_{E_{0}})^{K}

such that

\theta_{\varphi_{f}\otimes\varphi_{\infty}}(\alpha)\neq 0.

By Lemma
3.4.3
, this means that

0\neq\alpha(Z(T,\varphi_{f})_{K})\in O_{E_{0}}

for some
T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
.
Now we note that
\alpha
has the Hecke eigenvalues of
\pi
away from
S
by construction. In particular, for all primes
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
,
\alpha
lies in
\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,O_{\mathfrak{p}})
,
and

0\neq\alpha(Z(T,\varphi_{f})_{K})\in\lambda^{D}(1,\varphi_{f})_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\lambda^{D}(1)_{\mathfrak{p}}.

Since
\alpha(Z(T,\varphi_{f})_{K})\not\equiv 0\pmod{\mathfrak{p}}
for all but finitely many
\mathfrak{p}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.4.6
.

Suppose
D\geq 1
is squarefree and
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
.

(1)

For all admissible
Q
coprime to
D
with

\sigma(DQ)
even and all

\ell\not\in S\cup\div(DQ)
, we have

\Res_{\ell}\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q)\subset H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T_{\pi,n}).

(2)

If
\sigma(D)
is even, then we have

\kappa^{D}(1)\subset H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi}).

Proof.

Write
Q=1
in case (
2
).
We claim that, for all
S
-level structures
K
and all
z\in\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ}))
,

(4.4)

\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(z)\in H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}).

Note here that
H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
p
-torsion-free by Lemma
4.1.7
and Theorem
2.7.5
(
2
), so the Bloch-Kato Selmer group is defined as in Notation
1.5.2
(
1
).
Observe as well that (
4.4
) implies the proposition: indeed, it clearly implies (
2
) by the functoriality of the Bloch-Kato local conditions; and it also implies (
1
) by Remark
1.5.3
and
Proposition
1.5.5
.

Now note that, for all primes
\ell
,

H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{0,\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}
is pure of weight one as a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-module by Corollary
2.7.7
combined with Theorem
2.2.10
(
1
); hence

H^{1}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{0,\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)=0

for all
\ell\neq p

and

H^{1}_{g}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{p},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{0,\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)=H^{1}_{f}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{p},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},E_{0,\mathfrak{p}}(2))_{\mathfrak{m}}\right).

Thus (
4.4
) follows from

[
82
, Theorem 5.9]
combined with the proof of
[
80
, Theorem 3.1(ii)]
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.7
.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree, and let
Q\geq 1
be admissible and coprime to
D
. Then:

(1)

If
\sigma(DQ)
is even and
\mathfrak{p}
satisfies Assumption
4.1.1
, then
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q)
is generated by
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q;K)
as
K
ranges over
S
-tidy level structures for
\spin(V_{DQ})
.

(2)

If
\sigma(DQ)
is odd and
p>5
, then
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q)
is generated by
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q;K)
as
K
ranges over
S
-tidy level structures for
\spin(V_{DQ})
.

Proof.

We prove the first statement, as the two are similar. Let
K
be an
S
-level structure for
\spin(V_{DQ})
, and note that
K_{2}=K_{\ell=2}
has pro-order prime to
p
.
9
9

9

To see this, one first observes that
K_{2}
stabilizes some lattice
L\subset V_{DQ}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{2}
such that
2L\subset L^{\vee}\subset L
, where
L^{\vee}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-linear dual.
One obtains a natural map
f:K_{2}\to\SO(L^{\vee}/2L)\times\SO(L/L^{\vee})
, where
L^{\vee}/2L
and
L/L^{\vee}
are naturally nondegenerate symmetric spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
of dimension at most 5. Since
p>5
by Remark
4.1.2
, the image of
f
then has order prime to
p
, and the kernel of
f
is clearly pro-
2
.

After fixing a sufficiently small compact open subgroup
K_{2}^{\prime}\subset K_{2}
, we can ensure that
K^{\prime}\coloneqq K_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\prod_{\ell\neq 2}(K_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}^{\times})
is neat; it is then clearly
S
-tidy as well. Let
\pr_{K,K^{\prime}\cap K}:\Sh_{K^{\prime}\cap K}(V_{DQ})\to\Sh_{K}(V_{DQ})

and
\pr_{K^{\prime},K^{\prime}\cap K}:\Sh_{K^{\prime}\cap K}(V_{DQ})\to\Sh_{K^{\prime}}(V_{DQ})
be the natural maps.
For
R=O
or
O/\varpi^{n}
and
\alpha_{K}\in\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,R)
, we set

\alpha_{K^{\prime}}\coloneqq\frac{\alpha_{K}\circ\pr_{K,K^{\prime}\cap K,\ast}\circ\pr_{K^{\prime},K^{\prime}\cap K}^{\ast}}{[K:K^{\prime}\cap K]}\in\Test_{K^{\prime}}(V_{DQ},\pi,R).

If
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)
is fixed by
K
, it is also fixed by
K^{\prime}
, and we have
\alpha_{K^{\prime},\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{K^{\prime}})=\alpha_{K,\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{K})
by Proposition
3.1.4
; the lemma follows.
∎

Finally, we introduce some notation that will be used in the endoscopic case.

Notation 4.4.8
.

Suppose
\pi
is endoscopic associated to a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
.
Then for any squarefree
D\geq 1
with
\sigma(D)

even
, any
S
-level structure
K
for
\spin(V_{D})
, any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K}
, and
j=1
or 2, we define
\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)^{(j)}\subset H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi_{j}})

as the image of
\kappa^{D}(1)
under the natural projection

H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})\to H^{1}_{f}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi_{j}}).
We similarly write
\kappa^{D}(1;K)^{(j)}
and
\kappa^{D}(1)^{(j)}
.
It is easy to check
that

\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)=\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)^{(1)}\oplus\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)^{(2)},

etc. As usual, a subscript
\mathfrak{p}
is included when necessary for clarity.

5.
Nonvanishing criteria for changing test functions

5.1.
Setup and notation

5.1.1.

Fix a prime
q
, and let
k=\mathbb{C}
or
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, for an odd prime
p\neq q
. In the latter case we also assume fixed an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
, and assume throughout this section that

(5.1)

q-1\neq 0\text{ in }k.

We denote by
|\cdot|^{1/2}:\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
the unramified character such that
|q|^{1/2}=q^{-1/2}\in k^{\times}
, using the isomorphism
\iota
when
k=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

5.1.2.

Let
V_{m}

be the split quadratic space of dimension
2m+1

over
\mathbb{Q}_{q}
, with basis
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,v_{m}^{\ast}

and pairing given by:

v_{i}\cdot v_{j}^{\ast}=\delta_{ij},\;\;v_{i}^{\ast}\cdot v_{j}^{\ast}=0,\;\;v_{i}\cdot v_{j}=\delta_{i0}\delta_{j0}.

Then
L\coloneqq\Span_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\left\{v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,v_{m}^{\ast}\right\}
is a self-dual lattice
L\subset V_{m}
.
Abbreviate

(5.2)

G_{m}=\SO(V_{m})(\mathbb{Q}_{q}),\;\;G_{n}^{\prime}=\MP_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}).

For any parabolic subgroup
P\subset G_{m}
(resp.
P^{\prime}\subset G_{n}^{\prime}
), we write
R_{P}
(resp.
R_{P^{\prime}}
) for the normalized Jacquet module functor with respect to
P
(resp.
P^{\prime}
).

5.1.3.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
,
and consider the Weil representation on
\mathcal{S}(V^{n},\mathbb{C})
with respect to the localization, also written
\psi
,
of our fixed global additive character
of
\mathbb{Q}
(Notation
3.2.4
). If
k=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, we have fixed an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
, and
it follows from the discussion in (
3.3.2
) that
\mathcal{S}(V^{n},\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\subset\mathcal{S}(V^{n},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

is stable under
G_{m}\times G_{n}^{\prime}
; reducing modulo
p
, we obtain the Weil representation on
\mathcal{S}(V^{n},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
.
Whether
k=\mathbb{C}
or
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, we abbreviate

\Omega_{m,n}=\mathcal{S}(V^{n},k)
.

5.1.4.

If
\pi

is an irreducible, admissible
k
-linear representation of
G_{m}
, define
\Theta_{m,n}(\pi)
to be the
k[G_{n}^{\prime}]
-module such that

\Omega_{m,n}\twoheadrightarrow\pi\boxtimes\Theta_{m,n}(\pi)

is the maximal
\pi
-isotypic quotient of
\Omega_{m,n}
.
Similarly, if
\pi^{\prime}

is an irreducible, admissible, genuine
k
-linear representation of
G_{n}^{\prime}
, define
\Theta_{n,m}(\pi^{\prime})
to be the
k[G_{m}]
-module such that

\Omega_{m,n}\twoheadrightarrow\Theta_{n,m}(\pi^{\prime})\boxtimes\pi^{\prime}

is the maximal
\pi^{\prime}
-isotypic quotient of
\Omega_{m,n}
.

5.1.5.

Let

(5.3)

\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\xrightarrow{g\mapsto\overline{g}}\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}

be the double cover described in
[
35
, p. 1661]
, with canonical genuine character
\chi_{\psi}:\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\to\mu_{8}\subset k^{\times}.

5.2.
The structure of the Weil representation

Consider the Schwartz spaces
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)

and
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)
, viewed as representations of
\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\times\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}

via

(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot\varphi(x)=\varphi(g_{1}^{-1}xg_{2}).

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Fix a character
\chi:\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\rightarrow k^{\times}
.

(1)

The maximal quotient of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)

on which the first factor of
\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}

acts by
\chi

is realized by the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\chi\boxtimes\chi^{-1}

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}}\varphi(t)\chi(t)\mathrm{d}^{\times}t.

(2)

Assume
\chi

is nontrivial. Then the map in (
1
) extends uniquely to a map
f_{\chi}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\rightarrow\chi\boxtimes\chi^{-1}

via

\varphi\mapsto\frac{1}{1-\chi(g_{0})}\int_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}}\left(\varphi(t)-\varphi(g_{0}^{-1}t)\right)\chi(t)\mathrm{d}^{\times}t,

where
g_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}

is any element such that
\chi(g_{0})\neq 1.

(3)

For integers
a\leq b
, let
S_{a,b}\subset\mathcal{S}(q^{a}\mathbb{Z}_{q},k)
be the subspace of Schwartz functions that are invariant under multiplication by
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\times}
and translation by
q^{b}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Then for distinct, nontrivial, and unramified characters
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{m}:\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
with
m\leq b-a+1
,
f_{\chi_{1}},\ldots,f_{\chi_{m}}
are linearly independent as functions on
S_{a,b}
.

Proof.

Part (
1
) is elementary. For (
2
), we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\xrightarrow{\varphi\mapsto\varphi(0)}k\rightarrow 0,

which is equivariant for the trivial
\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\times\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}
-action on
k
. Since

\Home_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\times\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}}(k,\chi\boxtimes\chi^{-1})=0,

there is at most one extension of the map in (
1
) to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)
, and the formula given in (
2
) exhibits it.

For (
3
), let
x_{i}=\chi_{i}(q)
. A direct calculation shows that, for
\varphi\in S_{a,b}
,

\begin{split}f_{\chi_{i}}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{1-x_{i}}\operatorname{vol}(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\times})\bigg(\varphi(q^{a})(x_{i}^{a}-x_{i}^{a+1})+\varphi(q^{a+1})(x_{i}^{a+1}-x_{i}^{a+2})\\
+\ldots+\varphi(q^{b-1})(x_{i}^{b-1}-x_{i}^{b})+\varphi(q^{b})x_{i}^{b}\bigg).\end{split}

So it suffices to show that the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{a}-x_{1}^{a+1}&\cdots&x_{1}^{b-1}-x_{1}^{b}&x_{1}^{b}\\
x_{2}^{a}-x_{2}^{a+1}&\cdots&x_{2}^{b-1}-x_{2}^{b}&x_{2}^{b}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
x_{m}^{a}-x_{m}^{a+1}&\cdots&x_{m}^{b-1}-x_{m}^{b}&x_{m}^{b}\end{pmatrix}

is nondegenerate, and this follows from the Vandermonde determinant formula.

∎

5.2.2.

Assuming that
m\geq 1
, let

P=MN\subset G_{m}

be the parabolic subgroup stabilizing the isotropic line
\langle v_{1}\rangle
. Then
M

is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\times G_{m-1}
; we normalize the isomorphism such that

(5.4)

(\alpha,g)\cdot v_{1}=\alpha v_{1},\;\text{for}\;(\alpha,g)\in M.

Similarly, let
P^{\prime}=M^{\prime}N^{\prime}\subset G_{n}^{\prime}

be the preimage of the stabilizer of
e_{1}\in W_{2n}
(Notation
3.2.1
(
1
)); then
M^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times_{\mu_{2}}G^{\prime}_{n-1}.

We normalize the isomorphism so that

(5.5)

(\alpha,g)\cdot e_{1}=\overline{\alpha}e_{1},\;\text{for}\;(\alpha,g)\in M^{\prime}.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

(1)

The normalized Jacquet module
R_{P}(\Omega_{m,n})

fits into an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\Induction_{M\times P^{\prime}}^{M\times G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)\boxtimes\Omega_{m-1,n-1}\rightarrow R_{P}(\Omega_{m,n})\rightarrow|\cdot|^{\frac{2n-2m+1}{2}}\Omega_{m-1,n}\rightarrow 0,

where
|\cdot|

is the canonical character of
M
, and
\chi_{\psi}\cdot\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)

is a
\GL_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-module with action defined by
(g,h)\cdot\varphi(t)=\chi_{\psi}(h)\varphi(g^{-1}t\overline{h}).

(2)

Similarly, the normalized Jacquet module
R_{P^{\prime}}(\Omega_{m,n})

fits into a canonical exact sequence

0\rightarrow\Induction_{P\times M^{\prime}}^{G_{m}\times M^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times},k)\boxtimes\Omega_{m-1,n-1}\rightarrow R_{P^{\prime}}(\Omega_{m,n})\rightarrow\chi_{\psi}|\cdot|^{\frac{2m-2n+1}{2}}\boxtimes\Omega_{m,n-1}\rightarrow 0.

Proof.

When
k=\mathbb{C}
, this is
[
54
, Theorem 2.8]
; see also
[
35
, Proposition 7.3]
for our more convenient normalizations. When
k=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, the proof in
[
54
]
applies without change because
p\neq q
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.4
.

(1)

Let
\pi_{m-1}

be an irreducible admissible representation of
G_{m-1}
, and let
\chi_{0}:\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\to k^{\times}
be a character with

\chi_{0}\neq|\cdot|^{\frac{2n-2m+1}{2}}.

Then for all admissible
k[G^{\prime}_{n}]
-modules
\mathcal{M}
,

\Home_{G_{m}\times G_{n}^{\prime}}\left(\Omega_{m,n},\left(\Induction_{P}^{G_{m}}\chi_{0}\boxtimes\pi_{m-1}\right)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}\right)=\Home_{G_{n}^{\prime}}\left(\Induction_{P^{\prime}}^{G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\chi_{0}^{-1}\boxtimes\Theta_{m-1,n-1}(\pi_{m-1}),\mathcal{M}\right).

In particular, if

\pi_{m}\coloneqq\Induction_{P}^{G_{m}}\chi_{0}\boxtimes\pi_{m-1}

is irreducible, then

\Theta_{m,n}(\pi_{m})=\Induction_{P^{\prime}}^{G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\chi_{0}^{-1}\boxtimes\Theta_{m-1,n-1}(\pi_{m-1}).

(2)

Similarly, let
\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}

be an irreducible admissible genuine representation of
G^{\prime}_{n-1}

and
\chi_{0}\neq|\cdot|^{\frac{2m-2n+1}{2}}

a character of
\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}
.
Then for all
k[G_{m}]
-modules
\mathcal{M}
,

\Home_{G_{m}\times G_{n}^{\prime}}\left(\Omega_{m,n},\mathcal{M}\boxtimes\left(\Induction_{P^{\prime}}^{G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\chi_{0}\boxtimes\pi_{n-1}^{\prime}\right)\right)=\Home_{G_{m}}\left(\Induction_{P}^{G_{m}}\chi_{0}^{-1}\boxtimes\Theta_{m-1,n-1}(\pi_{n-1}^{\prime}),\mathcal{M}\right).

In particular, if

\pi^{\prime}_{n}\coloneqq\Induction_{P^{\prime}}^{G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\chi_{0}\boxtimes\pi_{n-1}^{\prime}

is irreducible, then

\Theta_{n,m}(\pi^{\prime}_{n})=\Induction_{P}^{G_{m}}\chi_{0}^{-1}\boxtimes\Theta_{n-1,m-1}(\pi^{\prime}_{n-1}).

Proof.

This is immediate from Lemma
5.2.3
and Lemma
5.2.1
(
1
).
∎

5.3.
Principal series over
k
for orthogonal and metaplectic groups

Some of the arguments in this subsection were inspired by the work of Zorn
[
130
]
. The results are new only if
k=\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

5.3.1.

Continuing the notation of (
5.1.2
), we now assume
m=n
.

Notation 5.3.2
.

(1)

Let
B\subset G_{n}

be the stabilizer of the maximal isotropic flag

(5.6)

0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\cdots\subset\langle v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\rangle

and let
B^{\prime}\subset G^{\prime}_{n}

be the preimage of the stabilizer of the maximal isotropic flag

0\subset\langle e_{1}\rangle\subset\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle\subset\cdots\subset\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle.

(2)

The Levi factor
T

of
B

is identified with
(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}

via its action on the associated graded of (
5.6
), and similarly the Levi factor
T^{\prime}

of
B^{\prime}

is identified with

\underbrace{\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times_{\mu_{2}}\cdots\times_{\mu_{2}}\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}_{n\;\text{times}}.

(3)

For any character

\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times},

we define (normalized) principal series representations

I(\chi)=\Induction_{B}^{G_{n}}\chi,\;\;I^{\prime}(\chi)=\Induction_{B^{\prime}}^{G_{n}^{\prime}}\chi_{\psi}\cdot\chi.

(4)

We write
W=S_{n}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}
for the Weyl group of
T
in
G_{n}
, which is also the Weyl group of
T^{\prime}
in
G_{n}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

The semi-simplified normalized Jacquet modules are

R_{B}(I(\chi))^{{ss}}=\oplus_{w\in W}\chi^{w},\;\;\;R_{B^{\prime}}(I^{\prime}(\chi))^{{ss}}=\oplus_{w\in W}\chi^{w}\chi_{\psi}.

Proof.

Over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, this follows from the well-known result for
\mathbb{C}
; see
[
130
, Lemma 4.8]
. Since
p\neq q
, the
p
-modular case follows by the proof of
[
114
, Lemme 34]
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.4
.

(1)

Any nontrivial quotient
I(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi

extends to a nontrivial intertwining operator

I(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi\rightarrow I(\chi^{w})

for some
w\in W
.

(2)

Similarly, any nontrivial quotient
I^{\prime}(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\prime}

extends to a nontrivial intertwining operator

I^{\prime}(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi^{\prime}\rightarrow I^{\prime}(\chi^{w})

for some
w\in W
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{B}\subset G_{n}

and
\overline{B}^{\prime}\subset G_{n}^{\prime}

be the opposite Borel subgroups to
B
,
B^{\prime}
. Recall the “second Frobenius reciprocity”

(5.7)

\Home(I(\chi),\pi)=\Home(\chi,R_{\overline{B}}(\pi)),\;\;\;\Home(I^{\prime}(\chi),\pi^{\prime})=\Home(\chi_{\psi}\chi,R_{\overline{B}^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})).

In the orthogonal case, this is
[
115
, II.3.8(2)]
. Although the result there is only stated for reductive groups, the proof can be adapted verbatim to the metaplectic case. (The key technical points are the existence of arbitrarily small compact open subgroups admitting an Iwahori factorization, and the conditions in Lemma I.8.13 of
op. cit.
All of the fundamental results on Hecke algebras in Chapter I of
op. cit.
apply to general locally profinite groups.)

Returning to the proof of the lemma, note that

R_{\overline{B}}(\pi)\neq 0\iff R_{B}(\pi)\neq 0

and likewise in the metaplectic case. Hence it follows from (
5.7
) that any quotient
I(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi

extends to a nontrivial map

I(\chi)\to\pi\rightarrow I(\rho)

for some character
\rho

of
(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}
; by Lemma
5.3.3
,
\rho

is a Weyl conjugate of
\chi
, so (1) holds. The argument for (2) is identical.
∎

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Suppose
n=1
and
\chi^{2}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}.

Then
I(\chi)

and
I^{\prime}(\chi)

are both irreducible and
\Theta_{1,1}(I(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi).

Proof.

First of all,
I(\chi)

is irreducible by
[
114
, Théorème 3]
. Then

\Theta_{1,1}(I(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1})

by Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
). Since the intertwining map
I(\chi)\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})

is an isomorphism by irreducibility, we have

I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1})\cong\Theta_{1,1}(I(\chi))\cong\Theta_{1,1}(I(\chi^{-1}))\cong I^{\prime}(\chi).

If
\chi^{2}\neq\mathbbm{1}
, this shows
I^{\prime}(\chi)

is irreducible by Lemma
5.3.3
and Lemma
5.3.4
. If
\chi^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
, we instead use Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
,
2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\dimension\Home_{G_{1}^{\prime}}(I^{\prime}(\chi),I^{\prime}(\chi))

\displaystyle=\dimension\Home_{G_{1}\times G_{1}^{\prime}}(\Omega_{1,1},I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi))

\displaystyle=\dimension\Home_{G_{1}}(I(\chi),I(\chi))

\displaystyle=1

since
I(\chi)=I(\chi^{-1})

is irreducible. This shows that the intertwining operator
I^{\prime}(\chi)\to I^{\prime}(\chi)

is unique, so the lemma follows from Lemma
5.3.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}

be a character such that
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}

for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Then for any submodule
\pi\subset I(\chi)
, we have

\chi^{\oplus 2}\subset R_{B}(\pi)^{{ss}}.

Similarly, for any submodule
\pi^{\prime}\subset I^{\prime}(\chi)
, we have

\chi_{\psi}\chi^{\oplus 2}\subset R_{B^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})^{{ss}}.

Proof.

The orthogonal and metaplectic cases are identical, so we just prove the result for
\pi^{\prime}\subset I^{\prime}(\chi).

Let
Q_{i}=L_{i}U_{i}\subset G_{n}^{\prime}

be the rank one standard parabolic subgroup corresponding to the
i
th long root of
T^{\prime}
. Then

L_{i}\cong\underbrace{\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times_{\mu_{2}}\cdots\times_{\mu_{2}}\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}_{i-1\;\text{times}}\times_{\mu_{2}}G_{1}^{\prime}\times_{\mu_{2}}\underbrace{\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times_{\mu_{2}}\cdots\times_{\mu_{2}}\widetilde{\GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}_{n-i\;\text{times}}.

Let

\rho=\chi_{\psi}\cdot\left(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{i-1}\right),\;\;\sigma=\chi_{\psi}\cdot\left(\chi_{i+1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}\right).

Since
\pi^{\prime}

admits a nonzero map to
I^{\prime}(\chi)
,
\chi

is a quotient of
R_{B^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})=R_{B^{\prime}\cap L_{i}}R_{Q_{i}}(\pi^{\prime}),

so we have a nontrivial intertwining operator

R_{Q_{i}}(\pi^{\prime})\rightarrow\Induction_{B^{\prime}\cap L_{i}}^{L_{i}}\chi_{\psi}\chi=\rho\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi_{i})\boxtimes\sigma.

This is surjective since
I^{\prime}(\chi_{i})

is irreducible by Lemma
5.3.5
above. (By (
5.1
), we have
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}.
)
By the exactness of the Jacquet functor, we conclude

R_{B^{\prime}}(\pi^{\prime})^{{ss}}\twoheadrightarrow R_{B^{\prime}\cap L_{i}}(\rho\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi_{i})\boxtimes\sigma)^{{ss}}=\chi_{\psi}\chi^{\oplus 2}.

∎

Lemma 5.3.7
.

Suppose
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\to k^{\times}
is a character such that
\chi_{i}
are all distinct and
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
for at most one
1\leq i\leq n
. Then

\dimension\Home(I(\chi),I(\chi^{w}))=\dimension\Home(I^{\prime}(\chi),I^{\prime}(\chi^{w}))=1

for all
w\in W
.

Proof.

If the Weyl conjugates of
\chi
are all distinct, this is automatic from Lemma
5.3.3
;
so assume without loss of generality that
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}

for exactly one
1\leq i\leq n
. Then the stabilizer of
\chi
in
W
has order exactly two.
The argument is the same for
I(\chi)

and
I^{\prime}(\chi)
, so we consider
I(\chi)

in order to minimize notation.

Claim
.

Any nontrivial map
f:I(\chi)\rightarrow I(\chi)

is an isomorphism.

Proof of claim.

Indeed, if
f

has nontrivial kernel, then
R_{B}(\kernel f)^{{ss}}

contains
\chi^{\oplus 2}

by Lemma
5.3.6
. But
\chi

appears with multiplicity exactly two in
R_{B}(I(\chi))^{{ss}}

by Lemma
5.3.3
, so then
R_{B}(\image f)^{{ss}}

does not contain
\chi
, which is impossible since we are assuming that
f

is nontrivial. Hence
f

is injective, so

R_{B}(f):R_{B}(I(\chi))\rightarrow R_{B}(I(\chi))

is injective. Since
R_{B}(I(\chi))

has finite length,
R_{B}(f)

is also surjective. But then
\cokernel f

has trivial Jacquet module, which means
\cokernel f=0

by Lemma
5.3.4
. So
f

is also surjective.
∎

Now by the claim,
\End(I(\chi))
is a division algebra over
k
, and
also a
k
-vector space of dimension

\dimension\Home(I(\chi),I(\chi))\leq 2.

Since

k
is algebraically closed, we conclude

(5.8)

\dimension\Home(I(\chi),I(\chi))=1.

Next observe that there are no non-split extensions of distinct characters of
T
over
k
.
In particular, we may decompose

R_{B}(I(\chi))=\bigoplus_{\chi^{w}}R_{B}(I(\chi))_{\chi^{w}},

where
\chi^{w}

runs over the (distinct) Weyl conjugates of
\chi
, and (
5.8
) implies that
R_{B}(I(\chi))_{\chi}

is a non-split extension of
\chi

by
\chi
. The same argument applies to show
R_{B}(I(\chi^{w}))_{\chi^{w}}

is a non-split extension of
\chi^{w}

by
\chi^{w}

for all
w\in W
.
Now for any nonzero intertwining map

f:I(\chi)\rightarrow I(\chi^{w}),

R_{B}(\image f)^{{ss}}

contains
(\chi^{w})^{\oplus 2}

by Lemma
5.3.6
. Hence the induced map

R_{B}(f):R_{B}(I(\chi))_{\chi^{w}}\rightarrow R_{B}(I(\chi^{w}))_{\chi^{w}}

is surjective, in particular an isomorphism since both sides have dimension two over
k
.
Since
R_{B}(I(\chi^{w}))_{\chi^{w}}

is non-split, so is
R_{B}(I(\chi))_{\chi^{w}}.

Hence

\dimension\Home(I(\chi),I(\chi^{w}))=\dimension\Home(R_{B}(I(\chi)),\chi^{w})=1

for all
w\in W
.
∎

5.3.8.

To state the next lemma, we use the following explicit generators for
the Weyl group
W
:

(i)

The inversion
s

sending a character
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}

to
\chi^{s}=\chi_{1}^{-1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}.

(ii)

For
1\leq i<n
, the transposition
w_{i}

sending a character
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}

to
\chi^{w_{i}}=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{i-1}\boxtimes\chi_{i+1}\boxtimes\chi_{i}\boxtimes\chi_{i+2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}
.

Lemma 5.3.9
.

Let
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

be a character.

(1)

Suppose
\chi_{1}^{2}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
. Then

I(\chi)\cong I(\chi^{s})\;\;\text{and}\;\;I^{\prime}(\chi)\cong I^{\prime}(\chi^{s}).

(2)

Suppose
\chi_{i}/\chi_{i+1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}

for some
1\leq i<n
. Then

I(\chi)\cong I(\chi^{w_{i}})\;\;\text{and}\;\;I^{\prime}(\chi)\cong I^{\prime}(\chi^{w_{i}}).

Proof.

Once again, the orthogonal and metaplectic cases are identical. We give the proof in the orthogonal case.
For (1), let
P=MN\subset G_{n}

be the rank one standard parabolic subgroup such that the Weyl group of
M

is generated by
s
. Then

M\cong G_{1}\times(\mathbb{Q}_{q})^{n-1}.

Also write
\rho=\chi_{2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n-1}\rightarrow k^{\times}.

Then

(5.9)

I(\chi)=\Induction_{P}^{G_{n}}\Induction_{B\cap M}^{M}\chi=\Induction_{P}^{G_{n}}I(\chi_{1})\boxtimes\rho

and similarly

(5.10)

I(\chi^{s})=\Induction_{P}^{G_{n}}I(\chi_{1}^{-1})\boxtimes\rho.

By Lemma
5.3.5
,
I(\chi_{1})

is irreducible, with an intertwining isomorphism to
I(\chi_{1}^{-1}).

By (
5.9
) and (
5.10
), this induces an isomorphism
I(\chi)\cong I(\chi^{s}).

The proof of (2) is similar: let
Q_{i}=L_{i}U_{i}\subset G_{n}

be the rank one standard parabolic with Weyl group generated by
w_{i}
. Then

L_{i}\cong\left(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\right)^{i-1}\times\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\left(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times}\right)^{n-i-1}.

By
[
114
, Théorème 3]
applied to the
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-factor of
L_{i}
, we conclude

\Ind_{B\cap L_{i}}^{L_{i}}\chi\cong\Ind_{B\cap L_{i}}\chi^{w_{i}}.

(In the metaplectic case,
L_{i}

has a
\widetilde{\GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})

factor, to which we may still apply the results of
loc. cit.
with a twist by
\chi_{\psi}
.)
Then as above we obtain an isomorphism

I(\chi)=\Induction_{Q_{i}}^{G_{n}}\Induction_{B\cap L_{i}}^{L_{i}}\chi\cong\Induction_{Q_{i}}^{G_{n}}\Induction_{B\cap L_{i}}^{L_{i}}\chi^{w_{i}}=I(\chi^{w_{i}}).

∎

Definition 5.3.10
.

Let
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}
be a character.

(1)

We say
\chi
is
generic
if

\chi_{i}\chi_{j}\not\in\left\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1},\mathbbm{1}\right\}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
\chi_{i}/\chi_{j}\not\in\left\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1},\mathbbm{1}\right\}
for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

(2)

We say
\chi
is
almost generic
if
\chi_{i}\chi_{j},\chi_{i}/\chi_{j}\not\in\left\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1},\mathbbm{1}\right\}
for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
,
\chi_{i}^{2}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, and
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
for at most one
1\leq i\leq n
.

Corollary 5.3.11
.

Let
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

be generic or almost generic. Then:

(1)

I(\chi)\cong I(\chi^{w})

and
I^{\prime}(\chi)\cong I^{\prime}(\chi^{w})

for all
w\in W
.

(2)

I(\chi)

and
I^{\prime}(\chi)

are both irreducible.

(3)

\Theta(I(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi).

Proof.

(1) follows from writing
w

as a product of generators and repeatedly applying Lemma
5.3.9
. (2) follows from (1) combined with Lemmas
5.3.4
and
5.3.7
. Once we have (2), it follows from repeated applications of Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
) that

\Theta(I(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}).

Then (3) follows from (1).
∎

Definition 5.3.12
.

Let

\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q})^{\times}\rightarrow k^{\times}
be a character.

(1)

We say
\chi
is

level-raising generic

if:

(i)

For exactly one
1\leq i_{0}\leq n,

\chi_{i_{0}}=|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

(ii)

For all
1\leq i<j\leq n
,
\chi_{i}\chi_{j},\chi_{i}/\chi_{j}\not\in\left\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1},\mathbbm{1}\right\}
.

(iii)

For all
i\neq i_{0},

\chi_{i}^{2}\not\in\left\{|\cdot|^{\pm 1},\mathbbm{1}\right\}
.

(2)

We say
\chi

is
almost level-raising generic
if it satisfies
1(i)
and
1(ii)
above, and moreover:

(iii’)

For all
i\neq i_{0}
,
\chi_{i}^{2}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
; and
\chi_{i}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
for at most
i\neq i_{0}
.

Notation 5.3.13
.

For
\chi

almost level-raising generic, the set of Weyl conjugates
W(\chi)

is naturally divided into two subsets: those
\chi^{w}=\chi_{1}^{\prime}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}^{\prime}

such that
\chi_{i}^{\prime}=|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}

for some
i
; and those such that
\chi_{i}^{\prime}=|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}}

for some
i
. We denote these subsets by
W(\chi)^{+}

and
W(\chi)^{-}
, respectively.

Lemma 5.3.14
.

Fix
\delta=+

or
-
. Then for any
\chi^{w_{1}},\chi^{w_{2}}\in W(\chi)^{\delta}
, we have
I(\chi^{w_{1}})\cong I(\chi^{w_{2}})

and
I^{\prime}(\chi^{w_{1}})\cong I^{\prime}(\chi^{w_{2}})
.

Proof.

For any
\chi^{w_{1}},\chi^{w_{2}}\in W(\chi)^{\delta}
, we may write
w_{1}^{-1}w_{2}=s_{1}\cdots s_{k}

where each
s_{i}

is one of the generators in (
5.3.8
) and
\chi^{w_{1}s_{1}\cdots s_{i}}\in W(\chi)^{\delta}

for all
1\leq i\leq k
. The lemma then follows from repeated applications of Lemma
5.3.9
.
∎

Construction 5.3.15
.

For any
\chi

which is almost level-raising generic, there is a quotient
J(\chi)

of
I(\chi)

defined as follows. Let
Q_{i_{0}}=L_{i_{0}}U_{i_{0}}\subset G_{n}

be the standard rank one parabolic corresponding to the short root indexed by
i_{0}
. Then we have

(5.11)

L_{i_{0}}\cong(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{i_{0}-1}\times G_{1}\times(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n-i_{0}},

and by
[
114
, Théorème 3]
,
\Induction_{T}^{L_{i_{0}}}\chi

has a one-dimensional quotient

(5.12)

J_{i_{0}}(\chi)\coloneqq\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{i_{0}-1}\boxtimes\mathbbm{1}\boxtimes\chi_{i_{0}+1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n},

where
\mathbbm{1}

denotes the trivial representation of
G_{1}
.
We let

(5.13)

J(\chi)=\Induction_{Q_{i_{0}}}^{G_{n}}J_{i_{0}}(\chi).

To study the theta lift of
J(\chi)
, we first have the following calculation in the rank-one case.

Lemma 5.3.16
.

If
\mathbbm{1}

is the trivial representation of
\SO(V_{1})(\mathbb{Q}_{q})=\operatorname{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
, then

\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1})=I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2}),

with the corresponding quotient

\mathcal{S}(V_{1},k)\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}\boxtimes\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1})

induced by

\varphi\mapsto\left(g\mapsto\omega_{\psi}(1,g)\varphi(0)\right),\;\;g\in\MP_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}).

Proof.

Using the injection

\mathbbm{1}\hookrightarrow I(|\cdot|^{-1/2})
, we obtain by Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
) an embedding

\begin{split}\Hom_{G_{1}^{\prime}}(\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1}),\mathcal{M})=\Hom_{G_{1}\times G_{1}^{\prime}}(\Omega_{1,1},\mathbbm{1}\boxtimes\mathcal{M})\hookrightarrow\Hom_{G_{1}\times G_{1}^{\prime}}(\Omega_{1,1},I(|\cdot|^{-1/2})\boxtimes\mathcal{M})\\
=\Hom_{G_{1}^{\prime}}(I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2},\mathcal{M})\end{split}

functorial in
k[G_{1}^{\prime}]
-modules
\mathcal{M}
. In particular,
\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1})
is a quotient of
I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2})
. On the other hand, it is clear that the map in the lemma defines a nontrivial homomorphism
\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1})\to I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2})
, so we have a nontrivial composite

I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2})\twoheadrightarrow\Theta_{1,1}(\mathbbm{1})\to I^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{1/2}).

This must be an isomorphism by Lemma
5.3.7
, and the lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 5.3.17
.

If
\chi

is almost level-raising generic, then
J(\chi)

is irreducible and
\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi).

Proof.

First note that, by
[
114
, p. 44]
,
J(\chi)

is the image of a nonzero intertwining operator
I(\chi)\rightarrow I(\rho)
, where

\rho=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{i_{0}-1}\boxtimes\chi^{-1}_{i_{0}}\boxtimes\chi_{i_{0}+1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}\in W(\chi)^{-}.

If
J(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi

is a nonzero quotient, then we obtain, by Lemma
5.3.4
, a nonzero intertwining operator

I(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow J(\chi)\twoheadrightarrow\pi\to I(\chi^{w})

for some
w\in W
. However, Lemmas
5.3.7
and
5.3.14
show that this composite (since it is not an isomorphism) must coincide with the intertwining operator
I(\chi)\rightarrow I(\rho)

whose image defines
J(\chi)
. In particular
J(\chi)=\pi
. So indeed
J(\chi)

is irreducible. Then
\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\rho^{-1})

by Lemma
5.3.16
and repeated applications of Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
). Since
\rho^{-1}\in W(\chi)^{+}
,
we also have
I^{\prime}(\rho^{-1})=I^{\prime}(\chi)
by Lemma
5.3.14
,
and this completes the proof.
∎

5.4.
Explicit theta lifting over
k
for principal series

Notation 5.4.1
.

Given
\varphi\in\Omega_{n,n}

and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{Q}_{q}
, define

(5.14)

\overline{\varphi}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}}\varphi(t_{1}v_{1},t_{2}v_{2}+a_{1}v_{1},t_{3}v_{3}+a_{2}v_{1}+a_{3}v_{2},\ldots,t_{n}v_{n}+\cdots+a_{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}v_{n-1})\mathrm{d}a_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}a_{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}},

with
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}

as in (
5.1.2
) above.

A direct calculation shows that:

Lemma 5.4.2
.

The map
\varphi\mapsto\overline{\varphi}

defines a morphism of
T\times T^{\prime}
-modules

R_{B\times B^{\prime}}(\Omega_{n,n})\rightarrow\left((|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes n}\boxtimes\chi_{\psi}\cdot(|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes n}\right)\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{n},k),

where
T\times T^{\prime}

acts on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{n},k)

by

(5.15)

(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\times(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})(f)(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=f(x_{1}^{-1}t_{1}\overline{y}_{1},\ldots,x_{n}^{-1}t_{n}\overline{y}_{n}).

Definition 5.4.3
.

For any character
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi_{n}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

with
\chi_{i}\neq|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}}

for all
i
, we consider the following condition on
\varphi\in\Omega_{n,n}
:

(5.16)

\text{
There exists $g\in G_{n}^{\prime}$
such that $f_{\chi}(\overline{\omega_{\psi}(1,g)\varphi})\neq 0$, where}\\
f_{\chi}:\left((|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes n}\boxtimes\chi_{\psi}\cdot(|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes n}\right)\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{n},k)\rightarrow\chi\boxtimes\chi_{\psi}\cdot\chi^{-1}\\
\text{is the unique projection deduced from Lemma \ref{preliminary calculation for theta}}.

The map
f_{\chi}

also exists and is unique without the assumption
\chi_{i}\neq|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, so
(C_{\chi})

makes sense for all
\chi
; this is elementary but not needed in our applications.

5.4.4.

Let
K\subset G_{n}
be the hyperspecial subgroup stabilizing the self-dual lattice
L\subset V_{n}
(
5.1.2
).

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Let
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

be almost generic and unramified, and suppose
\varphi\in\Omega_{n,n}^{K}

satisfies condition
(C_{\chi})
. Also let
\mathcal{M}
be any admissible
k[G_{n}^{\prime}]
-module. Then, for any nonzero map

\theta:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow I(\chi)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}

of
k[G_{n}\times G_{n}^{\prime}]
-modules, we have

\theta(\varphi)\neq 0.

Proof.

Since
I(\chi)

is irreducible, Corollary
5.3.11
implies that
\theta

factors as

\Omega_{n,n}\xrightarrow{\theta_{0}}I(\chi)\boxtimes\Theta_{n,n}(I(\chi))\cong I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi)\xrightarrow{f}I(\chi)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}

for some map of
G^{\prime}_{n}
-modules

f:I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}.

Then since
I^{\prime}(\chi)

is also irreducible, it suffices to show
\theta_{0}(\varphi)\neq 0.

Now, by Lemma
5.4.2
, the map

\chi\mapsto\left((h,g)\mapsto f_{\chi}(\overline{\omega_{\psi}(h,g)\varphi})\right)

gives a
(G_{n}\times G_{n}^{\prime})
-intertwining map

F_{\chi}:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1})\cong I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi).

Since
F_{\chi}

is not identically zero, Corollary
5.3.11
shows that
\theta_{0}

coincides with
F_{\chi}

up to a nonzero scalar; in particular
\theta_{0}(\varphi)\neq 0

if and only if
F_{\chi}(\varphi)\neq 0
. Then because
\varphi

is
K
-spherical,
F_{\chi}(\varphi)\neq 0

if and only if there exists
g\in G_{n}^{\prime}

with
f_{\chi}(\overline{\omega_{\psi}(1,g)\varphi})\neq 0
, which is condition
(C_{\chi})
.
∎

Similarly, we have:

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Let
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

be almost level-raising generic and unramified, and suppose
\varphi\in\Omega_{n,n}^{K}

satisfies condition
(C_{\chi})
. Then, for any nonzero map

\theta:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}

of
k[G_{n}\times G_{n}^{\prime}]
-modules, we have

\theta(\varphi)\neq 0.

Proof.

Since
J(\chi)

is irreducible by Corollary
5.3.17
, the map
\theta
factors
as

\Omega_{n,n}\twoheadrightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))\cong J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi)\xrightarrow{f}J(\chi)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}

for some map of
G^{\prime}_{n}
-modules

f:I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}.

By Lemma
5.3.4
, we may assume without loss of generality that
f:I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow I^{\prime}(\chi^{w})

is an intertwining operator. Then by Lemmas
5.3.7
and
5.3.14
, we see that it suffices to show
\varphi

has nonzero image under the map

\theta_{0}:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}).

By Lemma
5.4.2
, the map

\varphi\mapsto\left((h,g)\mapsto f_{\chi}(\overline{\omega_{\psi}(h,g)\chi})\right)

gives a
(G_{n}\times G_{n}^{\prime})
-intertwining map

F_{\chi}:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}).

As in the proof of Lemma
5.4.5
, since
\varphi

is
K
-invariant, condition
(C_{\chi})

is equivalent to
F_{\chi}(\varphi)\neq 0.

Now project to obtain a composite

F_{\chi}^{\prime}:\Omega_{n,n}\xrightarrow{F_{\chi}}I(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1})\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}).

Now we observe that any
K
-spherical vector in
I(\chi)
has nonzero image in
J(\chi)
; indeed, by the construction of
J(\chi)
it suffices to show this when
n=1
, in which case it is clear from the explicit intertwining operator in
[
114
, p. 44]
and the assumption
q^{2}-1\neq 0
in
k
. In particular, we have
F^{\prime}_{\chi}(\varphi)\neq 0

if and only if
F_{\chi}(\varphi)\neq 0.

On the other hand,
F_{\chi}^{\prime}

must factor as

F^{\prime}_{\chi}:\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1});

the map
\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))=I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow I^{\prime}(\chi^{-1})

is unique by Lemma
5.3.7
, so
F_{\chi}^{\prime}

coincides with
\theta_{0}

up to a nonzero scalar. Hence
\theta_{0}(\chi)\neq 0

is equivalent to
(C_{\chi})
.
∎

5.4.7.

We end this subsection with a convenient shortcut that we will use to check condition
(C_{\chi})
in the characteristic zero, non-level-raising case.

Lemma 5.4.8
.

Suppose
k=\mathbb{C}

and let
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to k^{\times}
be generic and unramified. Assume that
\varphi\in\Omega_{2,2}^{K}
satisfies:

(1)

For all
x,y\in V
, we have
\varphi(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
.

(2)

\varphi
is supported on
L\times q^{-1}L
and invariant under translations by
qL\times q^{2}L
.

(3)

There exist elements
x,y\in V
with
y\cdot v_{1}=0
and
\varphi(x,y)\neq 0
.

Then there exists
\chi^{w}\in W(\chi)
such that
\varphi
satisfies condition
(C_{\chi^{w}})
.

Proof.

Let
w_{0}
be the Weyl element in (
3.3.1
); then

\overline{\omega_{\psi}(1,w_{0})\varphi}\in\left((|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes 2}\boxtimes\chi_{\psi}\cdot(|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\boxtimes 2}\right)\otimes\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{2},k)

is a unit multiple of the function

\displaystyle c(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{V^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}}\varphi(x,y)\psi\left(t_{1}x\cdot v_{1}+t_{2}y\cdot v_{2}+ay\cdot v_{1}\right)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\volume\left\{q^{-2}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right\}\int_{V^{2}}\varphi(x,y)\psi\left(t_{1}x\cdot v_{1}+t_{2}y\cdot v_{2}\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{y\cdot v_{1}\in q^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

By condition (
2
) of the lemma,
c
is supported on
q^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times q^{-2}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and invariant under translations by
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times q\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
Note that the conditions of the lemma together imply that
c(\mathbb{Z}_{q},q\mathbb{Z}_{q})\neq 0
. From this, we will deduce that
f_{\chi^{w}}(c)\neq 0
for some Weyl conjugate
\chi^{w}
of
\chi
, which will show the lemma.

Indeed, write
\chi=\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}
and
\chi^{w}=\chi_{1}^{w}\boxtimes\chi_{2}^{w}
for
w\in W
. Then

f_{\chi^{w}}(c)=f_{|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{1}^{w}}\left(f_{|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{2}^{w}}c(t_{1},\cdot)\right)

(where the functions on the right are defined in Lemma
5.2.1
(
2
)).
Now, because

c(\mathbb{Z}_{q},q\mathbb{Z}_{q})\neq 0
,
c(1,\cdot)
is a nonzero element of the four-dimensional
k
-vector space
S_{-2,1}
from Lemma
5.2.1
(
3
). Since
\chi_{2}^{w}|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}
takes on four distinct nontrivial values as
w
ranges over
W
,
we may therefore replace
\chi
with a Weyl conjugate such that
d\coloneqq f_{|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{2}}c(t_{1},\cdot)
is not identically zero. Since
d
lies in
S_{-1,0}
as a function of
t_{1}
, Lemma
5.2.1
(
3
) again implies that either
f_{|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{1}}(d)
or
f_{|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{1}^{-1}}(d)
is nonzero. This concludes the proof because
\chi_{1}^{-1}\boxtimes\chi_{2}
is Weyl-conjugate to
\chi
.

∎

5.5.
Applications to formal theta lifts

5.5.1.

For this subsection, fix the following data:

•

A quadratic space
V
of trivial discriminant and dimension
2n+1\geq 3.

•

A neat compact open subgroup
K=\prod K_{\ell}\subset\spin(V)(\mathbb{A})
.

•

An odd prime
q
such that
K_{q}
is hyperspecial.

•

A subring
R\subset\mathbb{C}
which is either
\mathbb{C}
, or a finite flat extension of
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
(embedded into
\mathbb{C}
by a choice of isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
).
In the latter case we assume the pro-order of
K_{q}
is prime to
p
. Let
\varpi_{R}\in R
generate the maximal ideal (so
\varpi_{R}=0
if
R=\mathbb{C}
), and
write
k\coloneqq R/\varpi_{R}
.

•

A character
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}

that is either almost generic or almost level-raising generic (Definition
5.3.10
and Definition
5.3.12
).

With these data, we make the following notation:

Notation 5.5.2
.

(1)

Write
\mathbb{T}_{q}\coloneqq\mathbb{T}_{\spin_{2n+1},q,R}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}\subset\mathbb{T}_{q}
be the maximal ideal corresponding to
\chi
; explicitly,
\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}
is the annihilator of the unique spherical vector in
I(\chi)
.

(2)

For any ring
A
,
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
denotes the trivial
A[K_{q}]
-module.

Lemma 5.5.3
.

We have

\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{T}_{q,\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}}}k\cong I(\chi)

as
k
-linear representations of
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
.

Proof.

Write

\pi_{\chi}\coloneqq\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{T}_{q},\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}}k.

Then we have a map
f:\pi_{\chi}\rightarrow I(\chi)
, sending the generator of
\pi_{\chi}

to the unique spherical vector.
The first claim is that
f

is surjective. Indeed, if
\chi

is almost generic, this is automatic by Corollary
5.3.11
. If
\chi

is almost level-raising generic, then
I(\chi)

has no
K_{q}
-spherical submodules: since taking the
K_{q}
-invariants is exact,
J(\chi)

is the unique
K_{q}
-spherical constituent, and it cannot be both a quotient and a submodule by Lemma
5.3.7
. So indeed
f

is surjective.

It remains to prove
f
is injective.
Because
\pi_{\chi}
is generated by
K_{q}
-spherical vectors, every
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-stable subspace
V\subset\pi_{\chi}
satisfies
R_{B}(V)\neq 0
; for instance, this follows from
[
115
, Corollaire II.3.5]
combined with (I.3.15) of
op. cit.
Thus it suffices to show
R_{B}(f)
is injective, or equivalently that

\dim_{k}R_{B}(\pi_{\chi})=\dim_{k}R_{B}(I(\chi))=|W|=n!\cdot 2^{n}.

To compute the dimension of
R_{B}(\pi_{\chi})
, note that
R_{B}(\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{k})=k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]
by the Iwasawa decomposition for
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
, and the action of
\mathbb{T}_{q}\otimes_{R}k
is the natural one under the Satake isomorphism

\mathbb{T}_{q}\otimes_{R}k\xrightarrow{\sim}k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]^{W}.

Since
k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]
is a finite flat
k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]^{W}
-algebra of degree
|W|
, we have

\dim R_{B}(\pi_{\chi})=\dim\left(k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]\otimes_{k[X^{\bullet}(\widehat{T})]^{W},\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}}k\right)=|W|,

as desired.
∎

Notation 5.5.4
.

For all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},R)
, let
\overline{\varphi}
be its image in
\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)
.

(Despite the conflict with Notation
5.4.1
, we hope that the meaning will always be clear from context.)

5.5.5.

For the next proposition, recall the notation on formal theta lifts from §
3.5
.

Proposition 5.5.6
.

Let
\alpha\in\Test_{K}(V,R)

be a test vector and
n_{0}\geq 1

an integer such that:

(1)

\Theta(\alpha,\varphi)\not\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}}}

for some
\varphi=\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},R)^{K}.

(2)

For all
h\in\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}\subset\mathbb{T}_{q}

and all
\varphi_{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},R)^{K_{q}}
,

\Theta(h\cdot\alpha,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi^{\prime}_{q})\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}}}.

Then for any
\varphi_{q}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},R)^{K_{q}}
such that
\overline{\varphi}_{q}^{\circ}

satisfies condition
(C_{\chi})
,

\Theta(\alpha,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\circ})\not\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}}}.

Proof.

For all
f\in\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{R}
, we can consider the convolution

f\ast\alpha\in\Test_{K^{q}}(V,R)

(notation as in (
3.5.3
)).
By Proposition
3.5.4
, the map

(5.17)

\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},R)\rightarrow M^{n}_{n+\frac{1}{2},R}

defined by

(f,\varphi_{q}^{\prime})\mapsto\Theta(f\ast\alpha,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\prime})

is
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\MP_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-equivariant. By condition (2) of the proposition applied to
h=\varpi_{R}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}
, the image of (
5.17
) is contained in
\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}-1}M_{n+\frac{1}{2},R}^{n}
. Abbreviate

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\frac{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}-1}M_{n+\frac{1}{2},R}^{n}}{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}}M_{n+\frac{1}{2},R}^{n}}.

Reducing modulo
\varpi_{R}
,
(
5.17
) induces a map

(5.18)

\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}

which remains a map of
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\MP_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-modules by Proposition
3.4.6
. Now note that condition (2) of the proposition implies (
5.18
) factors through the quotient

\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{k}\twoheadrightarrow\cind_{K_{q}}^{\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})}\mathbbm{1}_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{T}_{q},\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}}k\cong I(\chi)

(Lemma
5.5.3
).
By duality

[
115
, p. 96, Propriété (vi)]
,
(
5.18
) is equivalent to a nonzero map

\theta:\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})\otimes\mathcal{M}

and, for
\varphi^{\circ}_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},R)^{K_{q}}
, we have

\Theta(\alpha,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi^{\circ}_{q})\not\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi_{R}^{n_{0}}}\iff\theta(\overline{\varphi}_{q}^{\circ})\neq 0.

If
\chi

is almost generic, the proposition therefore follows from Corollary
5.3.11
and Lemma
5.4.5
. So assume instead that
\chi

is almost level-raising generic.

Claim
.

Let
\mathcal{M}

be any admissible
k[\MP_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})]
-module. Then every
map of
\spin(V)(\mathbb{Q}_{q})\times\MP_{2n}(\mathbb{Q}_{q})
-modules

\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})\boxtimes\mathcal{M}

factors as

\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes\mathcal{M}\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})\boxtimes\mathcal{M}.

Given the claim, the proposition follows from Lemma
5.4.6
, because
J(\chi)\hookrightarrow I(\chi^{-1})

is injective (cf. the proof of Corollary
5.3.17
).

Let us now prove the claim. Since the statement is purely local in nature, we resume our local abbreviation
\Omega_{n,n}=\mathcal{S}(V^{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},k)
.
The claim is also insensitive to replacing
\chi

with any
\chi^{w}\in W(\chi)^{+}
(by Lemma
5.3.14
), so suppose without loss of generality that
\chi=|\cdot|^{\frac{1}{2}}\boxtimes\rho

for some almost generic character
\rho:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow k^{\times}
. Apply Corollary
5.2.4
(
1
) with
\chi_{0}=|\cdot|^{-\frac{1}{2}}

and
\pi_{m-1}=I(\rho)\cong I(\rho^{-1})
. Since
\Theta_{n-1,n-1}(I(\rho))=I^{\prime}(\rho)

by Corollary
5.3.11
, we obtain an isomorphism

(5.19)

\Home(\Omega_{n,n},I(\chi^{-1})\boxtimes\mathcal{M})=\Home(I^{\prime}(\chi),\mathcal{M})

that is functorial in
\mathcal{M}
. So it suffices to show the claim with
\mathcal{M}=I^{\prime}(\chi)
. But in this case, (
5.19
) combined with Lemma
5.3.7
shows that there is a unique non-zero map

(5.20)

\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi).

Since
J(\chi)

injects into
I(\chi^{-1})
, we also have the map induced by the theta lift

\Omega_{n,n}\rightarrow J(\chi)\boxtimes\Theta_{n,n}(J(\chi))\cong J(\chi)\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi)\rightarrow I(\chi^{-1})\boxtimes I^{\prime}(\chi);

this must coincide with (
5.20
) up to a nonzero scalar, which shows the claim.
∎

5.6.
Main result on changing test functions

5.6.1.

Let
\pi
,
S
, and
E_{0}
be as in
Notation
4.0.1
, and fix a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
E_{0}
, which we suppress from all the notation in this subsection. Fix an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}
inducing the prime
\mathfrak{p}
, and let
R\subset\overline{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
be the ring of integers of the maximal unramified extension of
O=O_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

We apply the results of §
5.5
to study the behavior of
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)
and
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)
as
\varphi
changes locally at a prime
q\nmid Q
.

Proposition 5.6.2
.

Suppose
q
is an admissible prime. Then there exists an unramified character
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
such that
\chi
is almost level-raising generic, and the corresponding maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}\subset\mathbb{T}_{q,R}
contains the kernel of the Hecke action on the unique spherical vector of
\pi_{q}
.

Proof.

Because
\pi_{q}
is unramified with trivial central character, we have
\pi_{q}=I(\widetilde{\chi})
for an unramified character

\widetilde{\chi}:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, uniquely determined up to Weyl action. Write
\alpha=\iota^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}(q,1)
,
\beta=\iota^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}(1,q)
; then by Theorem
2.2.10
(
1
),
\rho_{\pi}(\Frob_{q})
has eigenvalues
\alpha q^{1/2},\beta q^{1/2},\alpha^{-1}q^{1/2},\beta^{-1}q^{1/2}
, which lie in
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
. By the admissibility of
q
, we may assume without loss of generality that

(5.21)

\alpha q^{1/2}\equiv q\pmod{\mathfrak{p}},\;\beta q^{1/2}\not\equiv\pm q,\pm 1,q^{2},q^{-1}\pmod{\mathfrak{p}}.

We define the character
\chi
to be the reduction modulo
p
of
\iota^{-1}\widetilde{\chi}
, and the conditions (
5.21
) exactly correspond to
\chi
being almost level-raising generic.
∎

Corollary 5.6.3
.

Suppose
Qq
is admissible with
\nu(DQ)
odd, and fix an
S
-level structure
K
for
\spin(V_{DQ})
. Let

\varphi_{q}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},O)^{K_{q}}

be a test function whose image in
\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})
satisfies condition (
C_{\chi}
), where
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
is the almost level-raising generic character of Proposition
5.6.2
.
Then for all
n\geq 1
and all
\varphi=\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K},
we have

\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\circ};K)\supset\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K).

Remark 5.6.4
.

The same corollary holds for
\kappa_{n}^{D}(Q,-)
if
\nu(DQ)
is even, but this version will not be used for the main results.

Proof.

Suppose
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)=(\varpi^{n_{0}-1})

for some
1\leq n_{0}
; without loss of generality we may assume
n_{0}\leq n
and that

\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\prime};K)\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi^{n_{0}-1}}

for all
\varphi_{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},O)^{K_{q}}.

Now choose a vector
\alpha\in\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},\pi,O/\varpi^{n})
such that
\alpha(Z(T,\varphi)_{K})
generates
\lambda_{n}^{D}(Q,\varphi;K)
for some
T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
.
Lift
\alpha
arbitrarily to an
O
-valued test function
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\Test_{K}(V_{DQ},O)
.
Recall
R\subset\overline{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
is the ring of integers of the maximal unramified extension of
O
, and let
f_{\pi}:\mathbb{T}_{q,R}\to R
be the character associated with the Hecke eigenvalues of
\pi_{q}
, so that

f_{\pi}(h)\in(\varpi)

for all
h\in\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}\subset\mathcal{\mathbb{T}}_{q,R}
.
Then for
h\in\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}
and
\varphi_{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},O)^{K_{q}}
, we have

\Theta(h\cdot\widetilde{\alpha},\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\prime})\equiv f_{\pi}(h)\Theta(\widetilde{\alpha},\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\prime})\equiv 0\pmod{\varpi^{n_{0}}},

so we may apply Proposition
5.5.6
to conclude. (Note that because
q^{4}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
,
p
does not divide the pro-order of
K_{q}
.)

∎

We now give an analogue of Corollary
5.6.3
in characteristic zero, which requires
Q=1
.

Proposition 5.6.5
.

Suppose
q\not\in S
is a prime such that
\rho_{\pi}(\Frob_{q})
has distinct eigenvalues. Let
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the unramified character, well-defined up to
W
-action, such that
\pi_{q}
is a constituent of
I(\chi)
.
Then
\chi
is generic.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Proposition
5.6.2
, using that
|\alpha|=|\beta|=1
because
\pi_{q}
is tempered by Theorem
2.2.10
(
1
).
∎

Corollary 5.6.6
.

Let
D\geq 1
be squarefree with
\nu(D)
even, and suppose
q\not\in S\cup\div(D)
is a prime such that
\rho_{\pi}(\Frob_{q})
has distinct eigenvalues. Fix an
S
-level structure
K
for
\spin(V_{D})
, and let
\varphi_{q}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{Z})^{K_{q}}
be a test function whose image in
\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{C})^{K_{q}}
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.4.8
, or more generally condition (
C_{\chi}
), where
\chi:(\mathbb{Q}_{q}^{\times})^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is the generic character of Proposition
5.6.5
. Then for all
\varphi=\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K}
,

\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)\neq 0\implies\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\circ};K)\neq 0.

Proof.

The argument is similar to Corollary
5.6.3
. First fix a vector
\alpha\in\Test_{K}(V_{D},\pi,O)
with
\alpha_{\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{K})\neq 0
for some
T\in\Sym_{2}(\mathbb{Q})_{\geq 0}
.
Because
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})
is torsion-free by Lemma
4.1.6
(
1
), we may choose a linear functional
\beta:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
such that
\beta(\alpha_{\ast}\circ\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}(Z(T,\varphi)_{K}))\neck 0
.
Let
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\Test_{K}(V_{D},\mathbb{C})
denote the composite map

\CH^{2}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D}))\xrightarrow{\partial_{{\operatorname{AJ}},\mathfrak{m}}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},H^{3}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\Sh_{K}(V_{D})_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}},O(2))_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\ast}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},T_{\pi})\xrightarrow{\beta}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathbb{C}.

Then
\widetilde{\alpha}
is Hecke-equivariant because
\alpha
is so.
Let
\varphi_{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{DQ}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{q},\mathbb{C})^{K_{q}}
be any vector. By the Hecke-equivariance of
\widetilde{\alpha}
, we have
\Theta(h\cdot\widetilde{\alpha},\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\prime})=0
for all
h\in\mathfrak{m}_{\chi}

(Notation
5.5.2
), with
R=\mathbb{C}
,
\varpi_{R}=0
.
We can now apply Proposition
5.5.6
to conclude.
∎

With essentially the same proof, we have the following in the endoscopic case:

Corollary 5.6.7
.

With the setup of Corollary
5.6.6
, suppose
\pi
is endoscopic associated to a pair
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
. Then for all
\varphi=\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{D}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{A}_{f},O)^{K}
and
j=1
or 2, we have

\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi;K)^{(j)}\neq 0\implies\kappa^{D}(1,\varphi^{q}\otimes\varphi_{q}^{\circ};K)^{(j)}\neq 0.

6.
The ramified
\spin_{5}

Rapoport-Zink space

6.1.
The moduli problem

6.1.1.

Fix a prime
q>2
, and let
\mathcal{O}_{q}

be the unique maximal order in the non-split quaternion algebra
B
over
\mathbb{Q}_{q}
. Suppose given a
q
-divisible group
\mathbb{X}

over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
of dimension 4 and height 8,
equipped with an action
\iota_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}\hookrightarrow\End(\mathbb{X})

and a principal polarization
\lambda_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathbb{X}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{X}^{\vee}

such that the Rosati involution
\ast

of
\End(\mathbb{X})

induces a nebentype involution on
\mathcal{O}_{q}
of

unit type

(Definition
1.2.9
).

Definition 6.1.2
.

Let
\nilpotent

be the category of schemes over
\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{q}

on which
q

is locally nilpotent.
Let
\mathcal{N}:\nilpotent\to\operatorname{Set}

be the functor
sending
S\in\nilpotent

to the set of isomorphism classes of tuples

(X,\iota,\lambda,\rho)

where:

(i)

X

is a
q
-divisible group of dimension 4 and height 8 over
S
.

(ii)

\iota:\mathcal{O}_{q}\hookrightarrow\End(X/S)

is an
\mathcal{O}_{q}
-action such that

\debt(T-\iota(\alpha)|\Lie(X))=(T^{2}-\tr(\alpha)T+N(\alpha))^{2},\;\;\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{q}.

(iii)

\lambda:X\xrightarrow{\sim}X^{\vee}

is a principal polarization such that the Rosati involution on
\End(X/S)

extends the involution
\ast

on
\mathcal{O}_{q}
.

(iv)

If
\overline{S}=S\times_{\breve{\mathbb{Z}}_{q}}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}

denotes the mod
q

fiber, then
\rho

is an
\mathcal{O}_{q}
-linear quasi-isogeny
